Executive Summary
This report documents the integral experiment evaluation and publication of the LLNL
measurements for IER-518 (HiMu), the joint high multiplication subcritical (multiplicity) experiment.
This experiment is a fundamental physics evaluation of subcritical configurations using the water-
moderated and water-reflected Seven Percent Critical Experiment (7uPCX) assembly at Sandia
National Laboratories (SNL) with the 0.855 cm square-pitch lattice and U(6.9)O2 fuel pellets. These
subcritical configurations achieve estimated neutron multiplications of 20 to more than 1000. The
experiment design and measurement campaign was a joint effort between SNL, LLNL, LANL, and
ASNR1, completed in 2022. This evaluation was reviewed and accepted by the International
Criticality Safety Benchmark Evaluation Project (ICSBEP) Technical Review Group (TRG) on April 14,
2026, and submitted for publication in the ICSBEP Handbook in August 2026 following subgroup
approval.

This evaluation introduces two novel benchmark quantities into the ICSBEP Handbook using the
Feynman-alpha (variance-to-mean) method: the prompt asymptotic value (Ysat) and the prompt
decay constant (αp). This extends the existing benchmark quantities available to fundamental
physics evaluations in the ICSBEP Handbook for the analysis of time-series neutron count data of
multiplying systems, including: the singles (R1), doubles (R2), and leakage multiplication (ML); and the
Feynman moments (Y2F and Y3F). Additionally, the analysis implements two novel techniques for
characterizing these benchmark quantities: the Bootstrap Method 2 to quantify the uncertainty in the
benchmark quantities; and the Backward Extrapolation Technique3 to correct for dead time in the
experimental measurements. While the analysis and benchmark quantities are novel, this evaluation
incorporates significant experimental description, uncertainty evaluation, and benchmark
specification from two previously published ICSBEP evaluations funded by the U.S. DOE’s Nuclear
Criticality Safety Program (NCSP): SNL’s LEU-COMP-THERM-078 (7uPCX with 0.855 cm pitch) and
LLNL’s FUND-LLNL-ALPHAN-HE3-MULT-001 (ISSA).

Table 1 summarizes the six cases and benchmark quantities (Y sat and αp). The benchmark quantities
are calculated by analyzing and fitting the distributions of the measured time series neutron count
data, which represents the fundamental measurements from these experiments. The uncertainty in
the experimental measurements is determined using the Bootstrap Method while the total
uncertainty in the benchmark quantities is based on a detailed analysis of 21 uncertainty
components4. The model simplifications in this benchmark are the same as used in LEU-COMP-
THERM-078 and FUND-LLNL-ALPHAN-HE3-MULT-001. The leading sources of uncertainty in the
benchmark quantities are the fuel rod pitch and fuel rod cladding composition for both Y sat and αp as
well as the dry well position for Ysat. Notably, these two components 5 are also the leading sources of
uncertainty in LEU-COMP-THERM-078.

Research A, 735 (2014), pp. 53-59. DOI: 10.1016/j.nima.2013.08.053.

in LEU-COMP-THERM-078. However, the evaluated uncertainty in the oxygen-to-uranium ratio in this benchmark was
reduced by an order of magnitude (from ±0.1 to ±0.01), based on expert judgement from the TRG.

For Ysat, all calculational results show a consistent underprediction. Additionally, ENDF/B-VIII.0
results for both MCNP® 6.3.0 and MORET 6 show an increasing disagreement (within 2σ) between
the experiment and calculation as the subcritical configurations increase in multiplication.
However, this increasing disagreement is not present in the MORET 6 with JEFF3.3. For αp, the
MCNP® 6.3.0 with ENDF/B-VIII.0 results show a disagreement outside of 2σ for the lower
multiplication configurations (Cases 4 and 6). However, the MORET 6 results with both ENDF/B-
VIII.0 and JEFF3.3 largely show agreement within 1σ.

  HIMU: HIGH MULTIPLICATION SUBCRITICAL MEASUREMENTS OF
  WATER-MODERATED SQUARE-PITCHED LOW-ENRICHED URANIUM
 DIOXIDE FUEL ROD LATTICES

IDENTIFICATION NUMBER: FUND-SNL-U-HE3-MULT-001

KEY WORDS: subcritical, Feynman-alpha, variance-to-mean, array, fuel rods, square pitch, low
 enriched, uranium, uranium dioxide, water-moderated, water-reflected, acceptable

1.0 DETAILED DESCRIPTION

1.1 Overview of Experiment

This subcritical benchmark experiment consists of neutron multiplicity measurements of a water-
moderated and -reflected low-enriched uranium oxide fuel lattice. The primary purpose of this exper-
iment was to produce time series neutron count data exceeding a subcritical multiplication of 20. This
threshold represents the upper limit of existing fundamental physical benchmarks for neutron multi-
plicity currently included in the International Handbook of Evaluated Criticality Safety Benchmark
Experiments. Additionally, the experiment involved an inter-comparison of various detector systems,
utilizing unique setups from Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory (LLNL), Los Alamos National
Laboratory (LANL), and Autorité de Sûreté Nucléaire et de Radioprotection (ASNR)1 to measure the
subcritical configurations [1]. This evaluation focuses solely on the six measurements performed by
LLNL, all of which are judged to be acceptable as benchmark cases.

This experiment is based on two existing benchmark evaluations2 : LEU-COMP-THERM-078, Rev.
0 and FUND-LLNL-ALPHAN-HE3-MULT-001, Rev. 0. The experimental configurations are vari-
ations of Case 2 from LEU-COMP-THERM-078, adjusting the number of fuel rods to change the
subcritical multiplication of the system. The main parameter varied between the experimental config-
urations is the number of fuel rods. The detector system for the LLNL measurements uses the same
3 He proportional counters and electronics as in FUND-LLNL-ALPHAN-HE3-MULT-001, combined

with a new data acquisition system. There was no external neutron source present during the experi-
mental measurements, only the intrinsic source from the UO2 fuel.

The experimental configurations use the Seven Percent Critical Experiment (7uPCX) assembly at
Sandia National Laboratories (SNL). The assembly has a 0.855 cm square-pitch lattice with fuel fab-
ricated from unirradiated 6.90 % enriched UO2 fuel pellets. This fuel has a long history in criti-
cal experiments performed by SNL, including variations in fuel pitch (LEU-COMP-THERM-078,
 1 Previously known as the Institut de Radioprotection et de Sûreté Nucléaire (IRSN).
 2 Significant portions of this evaluation are either partly or wholly based on LEU-COMP-THERM-078 and FUND-

LLNL-ALPHAN-HE3-MULT-001. Throughout this evaluation, the relevant sections from LEU-COMP-THERM-078 and
FUND-LLNL-ALPHAN-HE3-MULT-001 are referenced as footnotes prefaced with either "reproduced from" or "based
on". Here, "reproduced from" means that the entire section is a word-for-word reproduction from the respective evaluation,
with only minor changes, if any. By contrast, "based on" means that the section uses information from the respective
evaluation but required significant changes due to differences or additions for this experiment.

Revision: 0 Page 1 of 171

LEU-COMP-THERM-079, LEU-COMP-THERM-080, and LEU-COMP-THERM-102); use of par-
tial reflection (LEU-COMP-THERM-096 and LEU-COMP-THERM-101); and addition of diluent
materials (LEU-COMP-THERM-097 and LEU-COMP-THERM-111).

The experiment campaign was conducted over three weeks in June, July, and October of 2022 at
the Sandia Pulsed Reactor Facility (SPRF) located at SNL. The LLNL measurements were conducted
from July 18 to July 22 during that time. The execution of the experiment was a collaboration between
SNL, LLNL, LANL, and the ASNR. The experimenters and reactor operators included Gary Harms,
David Ames, Rafe Campbell, Beth Hanson, Jason Soares, Patrick Ward, and John Hall of SNL; Jesse
Norris and Samuel Varghese of LLNL; Nicholas Whitman, Jesson Hutchinson, Geordie McKenzie,
and Robert Weldon of LANL; and Wilfried Monange, Jean-Baptiste Clavel, and Aurélie Bardelay of
ASNR.

1.2 Description of Experimental Configuration

1.2.1 Design of the Subcritical Assembly3 – These subcritical experiments use a variation of the
7uPCX assembly used in the critical experiments for LEU-COMP-THERM-078. A schematic of
the critical assembly as it was configured for the LEU-COMP-THERM-079 experiments is shown
in Figure 1. The same assembly tank was used for the experiments in LEU-COMP-THERM-078
though the assembly core was modified. Figure 2 shows a view of the critical assembly configured for
the LEU-COMP-THERM-078 experiments, on which these subcritical experiments are based. The
assembly core resides in an elevated assembly tank that is connected to a moderator dump tank at a
lower elevation. When the assembly is not being operated, the moderator resides in the dump tank.
The core tank includes a smaller-diameter projection at the bottom designed to receive the control
and safety elements when they are lowered from the core (shown in Fig. 2). When the assembly is
being brought to critical or the desired high multiplication subcritical state, the moderator is pumped
from the dump tank into the assembly tank. The moderator can be released by gravity to the dump
tank through two large-diameter normally-open pneumatically operated dump valves. The core tank
projection normally remains full of moderator when the core tank is emptied. The temperature of the
moderator is monitored in the core tank by three type-K thermocouples at different axial levels near
the tank wall. The thermocouples were mounted on an aluminum rod near the core tank wall. The
temperature of the moderator in the dump tank is also monitored by a type-K thermocouple. A heater
is included in the dump tank to keep the moderator at a constant temperature set by a controller at
the assembly control system. During operation, the moderator is continually circulated between the
dump tank and the assembly tank. The level of the moderator in the assembly tank is maintained by
overflow into a standpipe.

 3 This section is based on Section 1.2.1, Design of the Critical Assembly, LEU-COMP-THERM-078, Rev. 0 .

Revision: 0 Page 2 of 171

 Figure 1. Schematic of the critical assembly used for the experiments. As shown, the core is
 configured for the experiments evaluated in LEU-COMP-THERM-079.

Revision: 0 Page 3 of 171

 Figure 2. Schematic of the critical assembly used in LEU-COMP-THERM-078, which is the same
 assembly used for these subcritical experiments.

Figure 3 shows a cut-away view of the critical assembly core tank with the moderator drained from
the core tank. The inset shows the core tank full of moderator as it was during the experiments. The
assembly fuel rods are described in detail in a subsequent section. The assembly fuel is supported in
the assembly tank by two 1 in. (2.54 cm) thick aluminum grid plates. A guide plate, used to align the
fuel rods in the assembly during insertion, is located above the upper grid plate. The grid and guide
plates are separated by aluminum support posts. The assembly core is situated in the tank to provide a
6.5 in. (16.51 cm) thick water reflector below the lower grid plate. The diameter of the tank provides a
radial water reflector around the assembly greater than 6 in. (15.24 cm). The assembly tank standpipe
is set to provide an upper reflector approximately 6 in. (15.24 cm) thick when the assembly tank is
full.

  Figure 3. Cut-away view of the critical assembly core tank from LEU-COMP-THERM-078. The
 252 Cf neutron source is not present in these subcritical experiments.

The assembly has one control and two safety elements of identical design. Each consists of a ganged
four-rod assembly that passes through the grid and guide plates. Each of the rods in these elements
has a B4 C-filled absorber section separated from a fuel follower by a polyethylene-filled decoupler
section. When each of the elements is fully withdrawn, the fuel follower is in the assembly and the
absorber is above the surface of the assembly moderator4 . The two types of elements are differenti-
ated by the way they are used. During measurements, the fuel-followed control element and safety
elements were fully withdrawn to their most reactive positions. The three control/safety elements
are attached to the control/safety element drives through electromagnets. The control/safety element
drives are supported above the assembly tank by the drive support.

Figure 4 and Figure 5 show photographs of two of the cores in the assembly. At the time that both
photographs were taken, the moderator had been drained from the core tank. Figure 4 shows an
overall view of the critical assembly core in the assembly tank during fuel loading of one of the
reported configurations. In this view, the control element is down and attached to the control element
drive. Both safety elements are down and the safety element drives are withdrawn to the top of the
picture. The guide plate is visible above the upper grid plate. The upper grid plate and the guide plate
 4 Figure 3 shows the two safety elements in the up position, with the fuel follower in the core, and the control element

in the down position, with the absorber section in the core. The control and safety elements are further described in
Section 1.2.9.

Revision: 0 Page 5 of 171

have checkerboard markings to aid in the placement of the fuel rods in the assembly grid. The guide
plate has each column and row in the grid pattern identified. The two dry wells that house the fission
chambers for the assembly Plant Protection System (PPS) are visible in the picture.

1.2.2 Reactor Room5 – The experiments were performed in the reactor building at the Sandia
Pulsed Reactor Facility (SPRF). A view of the critical assembly in the reactor room is shown in
Figure 6. The reactor building is a large thick-walled, steel-reinforced concrete structure with a base
in the shape of a cylinder having an inside diameter of approximately 30 ft (9.1 m) and capped with
a hemispherical shell. A large steel and concrete door is present in the wall. Inside the building, the
reactor room is lined on the walls and the dropped ceiling by 8 in. (20.32 cm) of gypsum. The 4 in.
(10.16 cm) of gypsum that is nearest to the concrete walls is borated. The floor is an 8 in. (20.32 cm)
thick concrete slab, the upper 4 in. (10.16 cm) of which is borated. The ceiling is about 12 ft 5 in.
(378.46 cm) above the floor.

 Figure 6. View of the assembly in the reactor room.

 5 This section is reproduced from Section 1.2.2, Reactor Room, LEU-COMP-THERM-078, Rev. 0.

Revision: 0 Page 7 of 171

1.2.3 Assembly Tank6 – The assembly tank, shown in Figure 3, supports the assembly and contains
the moderator during approach-to-critical experiments. The tank is cylindrical with a coaxial cylin-
drical projection out of the bottom to accommodate the motion of the fuel-followed control/safety
elements. The inside dimensions of the projection are 21.75 in. (55.25 cm) tall by 15 in. (38.1 cm)
diameter. The radial wall thickness and floor thickness of the projection are both 0.25 in. (0.635 cm).
All parts of the tank were fabricated from 6061 aluminum alloy.

The inside dimensions of the upper tank are 40 in. (101.6 cm) tall by 36.88 in. (93.6752 cm) diameter.
The upper tank is 6061 aluminum alloy. It has a radial wall thickness of 0.25 in. (0.635 cm) and a
bottom thickness of 1 in. (2.54 cm). The upper assembly tank consists of two welded sections and the
grid plate support ring. The lower tank section has a 1 in. (2.54 cm) thick floor that provides support
for the assembly tank. The floor accommodates the tank supports and has holes to connect to the two
moderator dump valves shown in Fig. 1. The floor also has a large central hole for the projection.
The section has a flange at the top with an O-ring groove used for connection to the grid plate support
ring.

The upper tank section is essentially a flanged tube. The lower flange is drilled to match the flange
on the lower tank section and the grid plate support ring. The upper flange is drilled to connect to the
support structure for the control/safety and source element drives.

The grid plate support ring fits between the upper and lower tank sections and has an O-ring groove in
the surface that mates with the lower flange on the upper tank section. The lower grid plate attaches
to the grid plate support ring.

The assembly tank is connected to two standpipes. One standpipe contains a linear moderator level
sensor. The other contains an overflow pipe that determines the moderator level. The assembly tank
also has a float switch used to indicate that the tank is full of moderator.

1.2.4 3 He Detector Dry Wells – There are two types of dry wells present in the assembly tank.

The dry wells described in this section are used to accommodate the four 3 He detectors, while two
additional dry wells described in Section 1.2.6 are used by the PPS. The dry wells for the 3 He detectors
are T6 temper 6061 aluminum alloy with dimensions reported in Table 1, based on the drawing shown
in Fig. 7. No as-built measurements of the dry wells were recorded. The bottom of the dry well tube
is welded shut with an end cap of the same material.

The positions of the dry wells in the assembly tank are shown in Figure 23 of Section 1.5.2. These

Section 1.2.5. Since the diameter of the drainage holes is larger than the diameter of the dry wells, the
dry wells were pushed to the edge of the drainage hole towards the fuel assembly, shown in Figure 8.
These drainage holes are at known positions within the lower grid plate at 60◦ angles from the axial
center of the tank. The dry wells were then affixed to the outside wall of the tank using brackets
which cuffed the dry well near the top and clamped to the outside lip of the wall. The dry wells
were vertically and horizontally leveled using a bubble level with respect to the grid plate, shown in

1.2.5 Grid Plates7 – The two 6061 aluminum grid plates support and maintain the spacing of the
fuel rods in the critical assembly, shown in Figure 10. A third guide plate, similar to but thinner
than the upper grid plate, is located above the upper grid plate. Both grid plates are 1 in. (2.54 cm)
thick while the guide plate is 0.375 in. (0.9525 cm) thick. The lower grid plate is circular, 36.5 in.
(92.71 cm) in diameter, and is supported by the grid plate support ring that is part of the assembly
tank. The lower grid plate has six 4.00 in. (10.16 cm) diameter holes equally spaced on a 28 in.
(71.12 cm) diameter circle to allow passage of the moderator when the dump valves are opened. The
upper grid plate is a 16.50 ± 0.06 in. (41.9100 ± 0.1524 cm) square with four support bosses at the
corners. The support bosses are rectangular projections of the grid plates and are visible in Figures
3, 4, and 5. The upper grid plate is supported above the lower grid plate by four 1 in. (2.54 cm)
diameter threaded aluminum standoffs that attach to the bosses. The standoffs maintain a spacing of
19.88 ± 0.02 in. (50.4952 ± 0.0508 cm) between the top of the lower grid plate and the bottom of the
upper grid plate. The standoffs are placed on a 28 in. (71.12 cm) diameter circle centered on the center
of the grid plates. Similar standoffs maintain a 0.260 +0.005/-0.000 in. (0.6604 +0.0127/-0.0000 cm)
spacing between the top of the upper grid plate and the bottom of the guide plate. The centers of the
grid and guide plates are located on the vertical axis of the assembly tank.

A set of three grid plates - lower, upper, and guide - was fabricated for the experiments. These plates

 7 This section is reproduced from Section 1.2.4, Grid Plates, LEU-COMP-THERM-078, Rev. 0.

Revision: 0 Page 11 of 171

have provisions for 2025 fuel rod positions in the 45x45 square-pitched critical array. The array has
a pitch of 0.3366 in. (0.854964 cm). The hole arrays are positioned on the grid and guide plates so
that the axis of the central hole is at the center of the plate. Thus, the vertical axis of the central fuel
rod position in the array coincides with the vertical axis of the assembly tank. The tolerance on the
absolute location of each fuel rod position in the grid is 0.005 in. (0.0127 cm). The lower grid plate
has 2013 0.5 in. (1.27 cm) deep holes bored in it to support and locate the bottom of the fuel rods. The
upper grid plate and guide plate have matching through holes to locate the top of the fuel rods. The
diameter of the grid plate holes is 0.260 +0.005/-0.000 in. (0.6604 +0.0127/-0.0000 cm). All three
plates have three through holes - square in shape with rounded corners - machined in them to allow
for passage of the four-rod control/safety elements.

The rows and columns of holes in the guide plate are marked for identification. The guide plate and
the upper grid plate are anodized in a checkerboard pattern to assist in identifying the grid locations.

1.2.6 Plant Protection System Detectors8 – Two cylindrical fission chambers that were part of the
Plant Protection System (PPS) of the assembly were placed in dry wells outside the fuel array. These
detectors are components of the safety system and were not used for experimental measurements. The
dry wells were fabricated from 6061-T6511 aluminum tubing 2.50 in. (6.35 cm) OD with 0.125 in.
(0.3175 cm) wall. The bottom of the tube was closed with a 0.250 in. (0.635 cm) thick welded 6061-
T6 or -T651 aluminum plate. The bottom of the tube is 5 in. (12.7 cm) above the top of the lower grid
plate. The detector tubes are surrounded by an annulus of polyethylene 11.82 in. (30.0228 cm) tall
with an inner diameter of 2.603 in. (6.61162 cm) and an outer diameter of 4.535 in. (11.5189 cm). The
bottom of the polyethylene is 5.3 in. (13.462 cm) above the top surface of the lower grid plate. The
mass of each of the polyethylene annuli was measured on a balance with the following specifications
given by the manufacturer: repeatability 0.01 g, linearity 0.02 g, readability 0.01 g. The average mass
of the two annuli was 2017.28 g. The positions of the detectors were measured during the experiment
and are reported in Table 31 of Section 1.5.2, with approximate locations shown in Figure 23. The

detectors were centered axially at the horizontal midplane of the fuel with the axes of the detectors
parallel to the axis of the tank.

1.2.7 Water Reflector9 – As noted above, the lower grid plate was supported in the core tank so that
the core was reflected on the bottom by a 6.5 in. (16.51 cm) thick layer of water. The bottom surface
of the water in the core tank is 7.5 in. (19.05 cm) below the upper surface of the lower grid plate. The
overflow standpipe was adjusted so that the surface of the water in the core tank was 6 in. (15.24 cm)
above the upper grid plate. At this level, the water surface is 26.88 in. (68.2752 cm) above the top of
the lower grid plate corresponding to a water height of 34.38 in. (87.3252 cm) within the assembly
tank. The diameter of the core tank was sufficient that the core was reflected radially by more than
6 in. (15.24 cm) of water for all cores. The surface of the water does not fully submerge the upper
portion of the fuel rods (polyethylene spacers), but the fueled sections are reflected by greater than
6 in. (15.24 cm) of water.

1.2.8 Fuel Rods10 – With the exception of the fueled sections of the control and safety elements,
the fuel rods in the critical assembly were all of the same design. The design of the fuel rods is shown
in Figure 11. The fuel rods were fabricated in 2004 at Sandia National Laboratories from existing
UO2 fuel pellets removed from "Pathfinder" fuel assemblies obtained from The Pennsylvania State
University. The fuel rods in the Pathfinder fuel assemblies were separated from the assemblies and
the fuel pellets were removed from the original cladding tubes and fabricated into new fuel rods using
3003 aluminum alloy tubing welded to end plugs of the same aluminum alloy.

The cladding tubes are welded to the lower caps and the weld was checked for leaks. Passing the
leak check assured that the water moderator would not enter the fuel rods. The material stack in
the fuel rods, starting at the bottom, is as follows: a 0.500 in. (1.270 cm) 3003 aluminum lower
cap; a nominal 19.257 in. (48.91278 cm) stack of fuel pellets; a corrosion-resistant steel compres-
sion spring 0.180 in. (0.4572 cm) outside diameter, 0.138 in. (0.35052 cm) inside diameter, 0.875 in.
(2.2225 cm) uncompressed length whose length adjusts according to the actual length of the fuel stack;
a 1.000 in. (2.540 cm) 6061 aluminum spacer 0.207 ± 0.010 in. (0.52578 ± 0.02540 cm) diameter, an
8.38 ± 0.02 in. (21.2852 ± 0.0508 cm) long high-density polyethylene spacer also 0.207 ± 0.010 in.
(0.52578 ± 0.02540 cm) diameter, and a 1.000 in. (2.540 cm) 3003 aluminum top cap. Table 3 lists
the axial locations of the interfaces between the fuel rod components when the fuel rods are installed
in the critical assembly.

Before the fuel rods were fabricated, the masses of 100 of each of the non-fuel components of the fuel
rods were measured. The mass measurements were made on a balance with the following specifica-
tions given by the manufacturer: repeatability 0.01 g, linearity 0.02 g, readability 0.01 g. The results
of the mass measurements are summarized in Table 4. The sixth row in the table gives the results
for 100 sets of all five parts. It can be seen that the variability in the mass sum is dominated by the
variability in the mass of the polyethylene spacer. The variability is attributed to the manufacturing
process used to fabricate the polyethylene spacers. The last (seventh) row in the table gives the results
for 100 sets of parts without the polyethylene spacers.

After the fuel rods were fabricated, the outer diameter of each fuel rod was measured using a high-
precision laser micrometer system. The system consisted of three micrometer heads and the hard-
ware required to position the fuel rods in the micrometer heads. The micrometer heads were located
to measure the fuel rod outside diameter at 6.4 in. (16.256 cm), 10.15 in. (25.781 cm), and 13.9 in.
(35.306 cm) above the bottom end of the fuel rod. This gave a fuel rod outer diameter measurement
at about the midplane of the fuel pellet stack and 3.75 in. (9.525 cm) above and below the midplane.
Each micrometer made two simultaneous orthogonal diameter measurements. For each fuel rod, a
measurement was taken, the fuel rod was rotated by 45◦ , and another measurement was taken. Thus,
the outer diameter of each fuel rod was measured at three axial locations in four azimuthal orien-
tations. The manufacturer’s specifications indicated that the laser micrometers had a resolution of
0.000001 in. (0.00000254 cm) and a repeatability of 0.000005 in. (0.0000127 cm). The bias in the
micrometer measurements was established using a pin gage standard with a calibration traceable to
the US National Institute of Standards and Technology. The diameter measurements had a system-
atic uncertainty of 0.000022 in. (0.00005588 cm) which is the sum in quadrature of the 0.000015 in.
(0.0000381 cm) uncertainty in the pin gage standard with the maximum in the random variations in
the measurement of the standard on any axis for the three micrometers. The fuel rod diameter mea-
surements were made in a number of sessions over the course of several months. The stability of the
measurement system was monitored by repeatedly measuring two 12 in. (30.48 cm) long pin gages
during each of the sessions. These measurements also showed that the diameter measurements had

Revision: 0 Page 17 of 171

a random reproducibility uncertainty of about 0.000030 in. (0.0000762 cm). Of the 2199 fuel rods
fabricated for the experiment, five were removed from service and not used. The average measured
fuel rod diameter for the remaining population of 2194 fuel rods is 0.249980 in. (0.634948 cm), as
rounded from the original data, with a standard deviation of 0.000086 in. (0.000218 cm).

The design documents for the fuel elements from which the fuel pellets were removed specified the
diameter of the fuel pellets as 0.207 in. (0.52578 cm). The outer diameter of a sample of 123 fuel
pellets, drawn randomly from the fuel pellet stock used in the fuel rods, was measured using one of
the laser micrometers described above. The average measured diameter was 0.20694 in. (0.52563 cm)
with a standard deviation of the 123 measurements of 0.00019 in. (0.00048 cm).

The fuel rods were designed to be supported by the two 1 in. (2.54 cm) thick grid plates. The lower
cap fits in a 0.5 in. (1.27 cm) deep blind hole in the lower grid plate. The top of the lower cap is then
aligned with the top of the grid plate to make the combination appear as a solid sheet of metal. With
the appropriate grid plate spacing, the top and bottom of the aluminum spacers in the fuel rods are
nearly aligned with the top and bottom of the upper grid plate.

1.2.9 Control and Safety Elements11 – The critical assembly has three identical fuel-followed con-
trol/safety elements, two operated as safety elements and one operated as a control element. Each
control/safety element occupies four adjacent fuel rod positions in the critical assembly. Each el-
ement consists of four B4 C-loaded absorber sections followed by four polyethylene-filled decoupler
sections followed by four fueled rod sections. These sections are joined into four-rod bundles by 6061
aluminum alloy bundle plates. The three sections use the same 3003 aluminum alloy tubing as the
fuel rods. Each section has 3003 aluminum alloy end caps at the top and bottom of identical design.
When a control/safety element is fully withdrawn from the assembly, the fueled rod sections are in the
core where they are designed to be neutronically identical to the other fueled positions in the critical
assembly. The design of the control and safety elements is shown in Figure 12. The design of the
lower bundle plate is shown in Figure 13. The design of the middle bundle plate, of which there are
two in each control or safety element, is shown in Figure 14. The design of the upper bundle plate is
shown in Figure 15. All of the bundle plates were fabricated from 6061 aluminum alloy.

The fueled section of the control/safety elements is similar to the fueled section of a fuel rod. The
3003 aluminum alloy cladding tubes and end cap material are the same as were used in the fuel rods.
In order to allow the elements to be lowered from the assembly, the lower grid plate has four-position
through holes at the control/safety element positions as described above. The end caps on the fueled
sections of the control/safety elements mate with a 6061 aluminum alloy lower bundle plate that fills
the hole in the lower grid plate. An 8-32 corrosion-resistant steel set screw 0.750 in. (1.905 cm) long
joins each fueled section to the bundle plate. Above the bottom end cap is a stack of fuel pellets and
a spring similar to those in a fuel rod. The length and mass of the fuel pellet stack are known for each
of the 23 fueled sections that were fabricated to the same precision as for the fuel rods. The relevant
data on the fuel pellet stack for the population of 23 fueled sections are given in Table 6. The total
mass of the UO2 in the twelve fueled sections used in the experiments reported here is 1303.07 g and
the total stack length is 584.7 cm.

The top end caps of the fueled sections are joined to the bottom end caps of the polyethylene-filled
decoupler sections through a middle bundle plate using the same set screws as in the lower bundle
plates. The length of the fueled sections is set so that, when the lower bundle plate upper and lower
surfaces are in line with the surfaces of the lower grid plate, the upper and lower surfaces of the middle
bundle plate are nearly in line with the upper and lower surfaces of the upper grid plate.

The decoupler sections contain 4.800 in. (12.192 cm) long 0.207 in. (0.52578 cm) diameter polyethy-
lene rods inside the same 3003 aluminum alloy tubes used for the fuel rod cladding. The average
polyethylene mass in the population of 24 decoupler sections is 2.531 g with a standard deviation of
0.037 g. The end caps on the decoupler sections are identical to those on the fueled section.

The bottoms of the absorber sections are joined to the tops of the decoupler sections through a middle
bundle plate. The same corrosion-resistant set screws are used. The absorber sections are filled with
boron carbide powder. Two lots of boron carbide powder, each with a different average particle size,
were mixed in equal parts prior to loading into the absorber sections. During loading, the powder
was compacted by vibrating the tubes. The loading procedure specified that the absorber sections be
filled to within about 0.3 in. (0.762 cm) of the top of the tube. The top caps of the sections extend
0.286 in. (0.72644 cm) into the tubes. Thus the gap between the bottom of the cap and the top of the
powder was small. The average boron carbide mass in the population of 23 absorber sections that
were fabricated is 26.37 g with a standard deviation of 0.22 g. After filling, the top caps were welded
to the absorber section tubes.

The top of each absorber section is joined to the upper bundle plate by a modified 8-32 socket head
cap screw 1.125 in. (2.8575 cm) tall. Table 7 lists the axial positions of the interfaces in the control
and safety elements when the elements are fully withdrawn from the assembly to the positions in
which the measurements reported here were made.

Whenever moderator is present in the core tank during the execution of a subcritical experiment,
the safety elements are held at their most reactive position with the absorber above the surface of
the water and the fueled sections in the assembly core. In this position, a large negative reactivity
is available to quickly shut down the assembly should the need arise. The absorber section in the
elements is also well away from the assembly core. When data are taken during the experiment, the
control element is also fully withdrawn to its most reactive position so the absorber does not affect

Revision: 0 Page 23 of 171

the system neutronically. In this position, the upper and lower surfaces of the lower bundle plate are
in line with the upper and lower surfaces of the lower grid plate.

Revision: 0 Page 24 of 171

1.2.10.7 Efficiency Measurement

The efficiency measurement was performed for 2 h (7200 s) on July 22, 2022, using the 252 Cf neutron
source documented in Section 1.4.2. Table 15 reports a summary of the detector measurements. Fig-
ure 17 shows the core loading pattern used for the efficiency and background measurements. During
the efficiency measurement, the central source location contains the source fixture, described in Sec-
tion 1.4.2.1, with the neutron source. The control/safety elements were fully withdrawn to their most
reactive positions, leaving the twelve fueled follower sections in the core.

1.3 Description of Material Data

1.3.1 UO2 Fuel13 – The fuel pellets used in the fuel rods were drawn from the fuel stock that was
removed from fuel elements obtained from The Pennsylvania State University. The uranium isotopic
data were measured for ten randomly-selected fuel pellets from the pool of fuel pellets used in the fuel
rod fabrication using a high-resolution multi-collector inductively coupled plasma mass spectrometer
(ICP-MS). The measured uranium isotopic data are given in Table 17. The uncertainties shown with
the mass fractions are the standard deviations for the ten measurements. The systematic uncertainties
were estimated by the laboratory that made the isotopic measurements.

 Table 17. Isotopic composition of uranium in 6.90% enriched UO2 fuel pellets.

 Uranium Systematic Uncertainty
 Wt.% (a)
 Isotope (Wt.%)(b)
                      234 U             0.02814 ± 0.00013                    0.00013
                      235 U               6.9034 ± 0.0046                     0.0069
                      236 U             0.06336 ± 0.00012                    0.00063
                      238 U              93.0051 ± 0.0046                        -
                       Total                  100.000                             -
 (a) The uncertainties given are the standard deviations of the measured values for the

 ten different samples.
 (b) The systematic uncertainties were estimated by the laboratory that made the mea-

 surements and are given at the one-standard-deviation level.

The oxygen to uranium ratio was not measured.

Metallic impurities were also obtained during the ICP-MS measurements of the ten fuel pellets. The
results of the impurity measurements are shown in Table 18.

 2
 deviations are reported for the measurements that were above the detection limit for the element. Measure-
 ments at the detection limits were not included in the averages or the calculation of the standard deviations.

 limit.

 of the reported detection limits when one or more measurements were below the detection limit. No value
 is included when all measurements were at the detection limit.

 all measurements were above the detection limit, no value is entered.

1.3.5 Water17 – The water moderator in the assembly was taken from the deionized water supply in
the facility. Samples of the moderator were taken during the experiment and archived. No chemical
analysis was done on the water samples.

The facility water is taken from the Albuquerque municipal water supply. The deionizer is fed from
that source. The Albuquerque municipal water system is divided into nineteen distribution regions.
The water quality is monitored in each distribution region. Table 22 lists the impurities detected in
the water for the year 2003. Both the city-wide average and the maximum level across the system are
listed in the table.

1.3.5.1 Water Temperature19

The water in the tank is constantly recirculated and kept near a constant temperature of 25 ◦ C using a
heater. The water temperature was measured at three different heights in the reflector of the assembly
with Omega Type K thermocouples. These thermocouples are mounted on an aluminum rod near
the core tank wall at heights of 5.5 in. (13.97 cm), 9.5 in. (24.13 cm), and 13.5 in. (34.29 cm) above
the grid plate. The facility is also equipped with a thermocouple measuring the ambient tempera-
ture of the room. The thermocouples are calibrated using the Omega CL24 handheld temperature
calibrator. Table 23 reports the temperatures for each of these thermocouples at the start and end of
each experimental measurement. Temperature variations remained below 1.0 ◦ C over the course of
all measurements.

1.4 Description of the Source

There was no external neutron source present during the experimental measurements, instead the
7uPCX fuel rods are the only source of neutrons. Following the experimental measurements, a 252 Cf
spontaneous fission neutron source was used to measure detector efficiency.

1.4.1  7uPCX Fuel Rods – The intrinsic neutron source of the 7uPCX fuel rods is largely produced
through spontaneous fission of 238 U and (α,n) reactions on oxygen in the UO2 fuel. As noted in
Section 1.1, the fuel was originally fabricated from unirradiated 6.90 % enriched UO2 fuel pellets.
The dimensions of the 7uPCX fuel rods are reported in Section 1.2.8 and the fuel composition is
reported in Section 1.3.1. Table 26 reports the total UO2 mass for each experimental configuration.

 22 This section is reproduced from Section 1.3.9, Boron Carbide, LEU-COMP-THERM-078, Rev. 0.

Revision: 0 Page 38 of 171

 Number of Total UO2
 Case
 Fuel Rods Mass (g)(a)
                                       1               1058              115022.1
                                       2               1056              114804.6
                                       3               1048              113934.9
                                       4               1032              112195.4
                                       5               1004              109151.4
                                       6                948              103063.2
 (a) Based on 108.7165 g per fuel rod, reported in Table 5.

1.4.2 252 Cf Neutron Source – A 252 Cf spontaneous fission neutron source was used to perform de-

tector efficiency measurements on July 22, 2022. This source was placed in a special fixture designed
to fit in a fuel rod location. The fixture allows the neutron source to be placed at any location in the
fuel lattice, depending on the needs of the measurement. For the detector efficiency measurement,
the source was placed in the center position of the guide plate. This neutron source was not present
during the experimental measurements for the benchmark configurations.

The neutron source was produced by Eckert & Ziegler Isotope Products, Source No. Q4-583 (Catalog
No. CF230230500U). Table 27 reports the activity and neutron emission rate from the certification
of calibration dated Apr. 15, 2018. Table 28 reports the 252 Cf composition dated Jan. 30, 2018. Both
tables also include the decay corrected values for the efficiency measurement on Jul. 22, 2022, docu-
mented in Appendix C. The certificate of calibration reports a total uncertainty at the 99 % confidence
level of 3.2 %.

 Date 252 Cf Only 252 Cf Only
 Total Cf Total
           Jan. 30, 2018(a)         20583               21276                  -               -
           Apr. 15, 2018            19500               20190              2262000          2264000
           Jul. 22, 2022(a)          6374               6924                744400(a,b)     745800(a,b)
 (a) Decay corrected.
 (b) Based on SOURCES 4C calculation, see Appendix C.

1.4.2.1 252 Cf Neutron Source Dimensions23

The neutron source in the assembly is a small double-sealed 316L stainless steel capsule (Type A3023
from Eckert & Ziegler) containing the 252 Cf spontaneous fission source distributed in a ceramic ma-
trix. The source is attached to a fixture designed to be placed in a fuel rod location in the assembly
grid structure. A drawing of the neutron source capsule configuration and dimensions is shown in
Figure 18. The neutron source fixture is shown in Figure 19. The fixture dimensions are such that the
source is located near the axial midplane of the fuel rods. The bottom (source) end of the fixture is the
bottom end cap, essentially a cylinder of 0.540 in. (1.3716 cm) long and 0.220 in. (0.5588 cm) diam-
eter that is drilled and tapped to accommodate a #3-48 steel set screw that is 0.313 in. (0.79502 cm)
long. The bottom of the source fixture and top of the source capsule are 10.047 in. (25.51938 cm)
above the top of the lower grid plate. A tube identical to the fuel rod cladding tubes (nominally
0.250 in. outer diameter, 0.014 in. wall) covers the top 0.254 in. (0.64516 cm) of the bottom end cap
and extends above the moderator where it connects to a handle that rests on the guide plate. The tube
is slotted at the ends so that it fills with moderator when the critical assembly is filled.

1.5 Description of the Detectors

This experiment uses four Reuter-Stokes RS-P4-1636-210 3 He proportional counters to measure neu-
trons. The detection is based on ionization of the detector fill gas following the 3 He(n,p) reaction,
which has a significant thermal neutron absorption cross section. The measurement is of the time and
channel at which neutron events are detected by the 3 He proportional counters.

The 3 He proportional counters are each operated by a PDT100A neutron pulse monitoring module25 .
The PDT100A modules are designed for high radiation environments and connect directly to the 3 He
proportional counters. These modules are charge sensitive amplifiers that produce a 50 ns digital
pulse, referred to as the "TTL OUT", each time a bipolar pulse, corresponding to a neutron event,
exceeds the set discriminator threshold. The modules are powered by a +5 V power supply and apply
a high voltage of 1100 V to the 3 He proportional counters, which is in the operating voltage range of
the typical plateau curve reported by the manufacturer26 . The "TTL OUT" is sent to the Advanced
List Mode Module (ALMM) and recorded by the Data AcQuisition System (DAQS) software.
 25 Application Note: PDT100A, Precision Data Technology, Everett, WA. URL: https://pdt-inc.com/products/

100series/100a/index.html (Accessed May 12, 2025).
 26 The GE Reuter-Stokes Specification for the RS-P4-1636-210 (dated Mar. 3, 2008) lists an Operating Characteristics

Voltage Range of 900 V to 1600 V. Personal communications with the manufacturer note that these specifications have
not changed since the design was initially released in 1998.

Revision: 0 Page 42 of 171

The ALMM is an advanced, high speed, multi-channel instrument for time-stamping neutron events,
developed by Los Alamos National Laboratory27 . The ALMM is capable of processing pulses on
multiple channels simultaneously with a time resolution of 100 ns. DAQS is the controller and data
acquisition software for the ALMM, recording the time series neutron event data. These measure-
ments use DAQS in list mode, storing the event data in the List Mode eXtension (LMX) file format28 .
At the time of these measurements, Version 2.03 of the LMX file format was being used.

1.5.1 Detector Dimensions29 – Figure 20 shows the 3 He neutron proportional counter used in the
subcritical experiments. Table 30 lists the specifications of the 3 He tubes.

The body of the 3 He proportional counters is made of 304 stainless steel. The specific material
composition of the stainless steel is not reported. The insulators inside the tube are alumina, the field
tubes are nickel, and the anode wire is tungsten. The fill gas is 98 wt.% 3 He and 2 wt.% CO2 . The
body of the electronics box is assumed to be aluminum.

The nominal overall length of the tube is 39.44 in. (100.1776 cm) and each detector has a nominal
active length of 36 in. (91.44 cm).

1.6 Measurement Techniques

The fundamental measurement from these experiments is time series neutron count data, reported as
(time, channel) pairs corresponding to the time and channel at which a neutron was detected. These
data are analyzed using the Feynman-alpha (variance-to-mean) method to infer the prompt decay
constant, αp , and the prompt asymptotic value, Y sat , which are presented as the benchmark quantities.
The measurement uncertainty is determined using the bootstrap method described in Section 1.6.1. A
correction to the measured quantities due to the physical dead time of the detection system is described
in Section 1.6.2.

The Feynman-alpha method involves a statistical analysis of the neutron count data to measure the
change in count rate due to the time correlation of neutrons produced through fission. This is done
using the variance-to-mean ratio of the count rate as a convenient measure of that correlation30,31 .
This ratio is useful because for a purely random source that follows a Poisson distribution, such
as radioactive decay, the variance-to-mean ratio is one. Therefore, any deviation from one would
indicate the presence of a correlated source, such as those neutrons produced through fission. The
variance-to-mean ratio of the count rate can be defined as

 c2 − (c̄)2
 = 1 +Y (1)
 c̄

  30 F. de Hoffmann. “Statistical Aspects of Pile Theory”. In: Science and Engineering of Nuclear Power. Vol. 2.

Cambridge, Massachusetts: Addison-Wesley Press, 1949. Chap. 9, pp. 103–119.
  31 R. Feynman, F. de Hoffmann, and R. Serber. “Dispersion of the Neutron Emission in U-235 Fission”. Journal of

Nuclear Energy 3 (1956), pp. 64–69. DOI: 10.1016/0891-3919(56)90042-0.

Revision: 0 Page 47 of 171

where c2 − (c̄)2 is the variance in the number of counts per unit time, c̄ is the average number of
counts per unit time, and Y is a measurement of the fluctuations in excess of the random Poisson
fluctuations30 .

The count rate defined in Eq. 1 must be binned in time using a bin width τ, referred to as the time gate
width. For a given measurement time T , the number of bins N, referred to as gates, is equal to T /τ.
The mean number of counts and variance in the number of counts for a given time gate width are then
defined as

 1 N
 c̄ = ∑ ci (2)
 N i=1

 1 N
                                                                                     c2 − (c̄)2 =          ∑ (ci − c̄)2
 N i=1
 (3)

where ci is the number of counts in the ith gate. These definitions for the mean and variance are
equivalent to the first raw moment and second central moment of the count rate distribution.

A useful method of visualizing this binned count-rate data is by constructing a histogram of the
distribution, as shown in Figure 25. The right plot shows the background measurement (τ=10 s) while
the left plot shows the Case 6 measurement (τ=100 ms). As expected, the background measurement is
uncorrelated and follows the Poisson distribution while the Case 6 measurement skews left, indicating
the presence of neutron counts correlated in time due to the prompt fission process.

  distributions are scaled to match the peak of the experimental measurements (i.e., by a factor of

Using a histogram representation of the count rate data, Eq. 2 and Eq. 3 can be rewritten as

 1 ∞

 N k=0

 1 ∞
                                                                  c2 − (c̄)2 =     ∑ bk (k − c̄)2                 (5)
 N k=0

where N is the number of gates and bk is the number of gates having exactly k counts, corresponding
to the frequencies and bins of the histogram, respectively.

In order to infer reactor parameters from the Feynman-alpha method, the dependence of Y as a func-
tion of τ is analyzed. When the time gate width is sufficiently short compared to the decay constant
of delayed neutrons or the contribution to Y (τ) from delayed neutrons is small, Y (τ) can be defined
as

 1 − e−ατ
  
                                                                  Y (τ) = Ysat 1 −                                (6)
 ατ

where Y sat and α are the prompt asymptotic value and prompt decay constant, respectively32 . With
this relationship, as τ → ∞, Y (τ) approaches an asymptote of Y sat . Figure 26 shows Y (τ) for the
experimental measurements using τ ∈ (10−4 s, 101 s).

 Figure 26. Y (τ) for the experimental measurements.
 32 R. Feynman, F. de Hoffmann, and R. Serber. “Dispersion of the Neutron Emission in U-235 Fission”. Journal of

Nuclear Energy 3 (1956), pp. 64–69. DOI: 10.1016/0891-3919(56)90042-0.

Revision: 0 Page 49 of 171

It is clear from Figure 26 that Y (τ) for these experimental measurements does not approach a prompt
asymptote as τ becomes large. This is due to the contribution to Y (τ) from delayed neutrons. For
typical values of these delayed neutron constants, the contribution becomes significant at time gates
greater than 0.1 s and increases in magnitude at higher multiplications33 , which was a goal of this
experiment. Therefore, when using Eq. 6, care must be taken to ensure that τ is sufficiently small
such that the Y (τ) is not influenced by delayed neutrons and yet large enough to have reached the
asymptote for the solution neglecting delayed neutron34 . Furthermore, the resulting prompt decay
constant and prompt asymptotic value are sensitive to the selection of the range of time gates if the
asymptote is not achieved prior to the influence of delayed neutrons.

Instead, Y (τ) is better represented by a sum of exponentials than the single exponential in Eq. 6,
representing only the prompt neutrons33,34 . While this sum of exponentials may be represented by
one prompt neutron group and one or more delayed neutron groups, a single delayed group is sufficient
to fit these experimental measurement results. Therefore, Y (τ) can be defined as

                                           1 − e−α p τ               1 − e−αd τ
    
                          Y (τ) = Ysat 1 −               +Ysat,d 1 −                                              (7)
 α pτ αd τ

where Y sat and αp are the asymptotic value and decay constant of the prompt neutron term and Y sat,d
and αd are that of the delayed neutron term35 .

Using Y (τ) from the experimental measurements, a least-squares fit of Eq. 7 can be used to determine
the optimal parameters for Y sat , α p , Y sat,d , and αd by minimizing the residual with respect to the
measurement. Figure 27 shows the least-squares fits for Case 1 and Case 6. Case 1 (left) is the
highest multiplication measurement performed in this experiment and has a significant contribution
from the delayed neutron term beginning around 0.1 s, while Case 6 (right) is the lowest multiplication
measurement and has a significant contribution beginning around 1 s.

The range of time gates used in the analysis of these experimental measurements is 10−4 s to 1 s for
all cases. The non-linear fit of Eq. 7 is performed using 29 time gates uniformly spaced in logarithmic
time, corresponding to seven points per decade, inclusive. This range of time gates and the number of
points is based on a rigorous convergence analysis documented in Appendix D.

1.6.1 Measurement Uncertainty – The uncertainty in the Feynman-alpha method is estimated us-
ing the bootstrap method36 . This method uses a combination of the bunching method37 and a resam-
pling technique to estimate the statistical uncertainty of a single measurement. This requires selecting
a basic counting gate width, T0 , and a bunching gate width, kT0 . This bunching gate width represents
the time gate width, τ, using k multiples of the basic counting gate width.

The reference in Footnote 36 provides a detailed procedure for applying the bootstrap method to time
series neutron count data. As previously stated, the non-linear fit of Eq. 7 is performed using 29 time
gates uniformly spaced in logarithmic time from 10−4 s to 1 s. Therefore, a basic counting gate width
of 10−4 s with k bunchings from 1 to 104 is used to perform the bootstrap method. A total of 1000
bootstrap replicates, B, were generated for each experimental measurement, as recommended by the
reference.

Figure 28 shows the bootstrap method applied to the 60 min measurement of Case 6 (left) and com-
pares it to the mean of that measurement split into six separate 10 min measurements (right). The
bootstrap method shows good agreement in the trend of the uncertainty as the time gate width in-
creases. As expected, the uncertainty of the single 60 min measurement is smaller than the distribution
of the separate 10 min measurements.

Nuclear Science and Technology 53 (2016), pp. 1447–1453. DOI: 10.1080/00223131.2015.1113898.
 37 T. Misawa, S. Shiroya, and K. Kanda. “Measurement of Prompt Neutron Decay Constant and Large Subcriticality

by the Feynman-α Method”. Nuclear Science and Engineering 104 (1990), pp. 53–64. DOI: 10.13182/NSE104-53.

The final step in the bootstrap method is to apply the least-squares fit of Eq. 7 to the B bootstrap
replicates, resulting in a distribution of the best-fit parameters corresponding to Y sat , αp , Y sat,d , and αd .
Figure 29 shows an example of the αp and Y sat distributions for Case 6. The parameter distributions
are approximately normal. Therefore, the uncertainty in the distributions can be approximated as the
standard deviation of the normal distribution. The method recommends weighting the least-squares
fit of Y (τ) by the inverse of the estimated uncertainty determined through the bootstrap replicates.
However, this results in instability of the fit parameters and was not performed for the analysis of
these experimental measurements.

1.6.2 Dead Time Correction – The dead time of a detector system is defined as the minimum time
separation between two events in order for them to be recorded as two separate pulses38 . This mini-
mum time separation may result in dead time losses as some events may occur too quickly following
a preceding event. The dead time losses in these experimental measurements are corrected using the
backwards extrapolation technique39 . This technique imposes a range of dead times on the experi-
mental measurements to determine the relationship between dead time and the inferred quantities (c̄,
αp , and Y sat ) from the measurements, with the goal of correcting these quantities to zero dead time
through extrapolation.

The reference in Footnote 39 provides a description for applying the backwards extrapolation tech-
nique to time series neutron count data40 . Assuming the measurements have a dead time bounded by
τ0 , the measured quantities can be described as a function of dead time τ by imposing a growing dead
time τ > τ0 on the time series neutron count data. Then, by extrapolating the moments to a dead time
of τ = 0, the measured quantities can be estimated in the absence of dead time.

This reference evaluates 2nd and 3rd order polynomial fits for the backwards extrapolation. However,
there is no significant difference between 1st, 2nd, and 3rd order polynomial fits when applied to this
experimental data. Therefore, a 1st order polynomial fit is used, defined as

                                                     y(τ) = mτ + b                                                      (8)

where τ is the imposed dead time, m is the slope, and b is the y-intercept. The m and b parameters are
determined through a least-squares fit. This y-intercept represents the backwards extrapolation of the
measured quantity to τ = 0.

Figure 30 shows the backwards extrapolation technique applied to the average count rate, c̄, prompt
asymptotic value, Y sat , and prompt decay constant, αp , of the Case 6 experimental measurement. Dead
times ranging from 0 µs to 10 µs in increments of 0.5 µs were imposed on the time series neutron
count data on a per-channel basis. The imposed dead times appear to diverge from the first order
backwards extrapolation around 2 µs to 3 µs, which indicates the true dead time of the detector system.
Therefore, the 1st order polynomial was fit to imposed dead times between 4 µs and 10 µs. This range
was consistent across the experimental measurements.

for a general description of dead time.
 39 T. Ridnik et al. “LIST-Mode Applications for Neutron Multiplicity Counting”. Nuclear Instruments and Methods in

Physics Research A 735 (2014), pp. 53–59. DOI: 10.1016/j.nima.2013.08.053.
 40 In this reference, the backwards extrapolation technique was applied to the singles, doubles, and triples rates of a

neutron multiplicity measurement. The singles rate is equivalent to the mean neutron count rate, c̄, and the doubles rate is
equivalent to the variance-to-mean ratio, [c¯2 − (c̄)2 ]/c̄, in Eq. 1.

method in Section 1.7. The R2 values for the prompt decay constant αp decrease with increasing
multiplication of the configurations from Case 6 (lowest multiplication) to Case 1 (highest multiplica-
tion), indicating a progressively poorer linear least-squares fit. However, the dead time corrections to
αp are not statistically significant when compared to the 1σ uncertainty determined by the bootstrap
method. On the other hand, the dead time corrections to the prompt asymptotic value Y sat all exceed
the 1σ uncertainty determined by the bootstrap method and show a consistently strong linear fit.

Additional figures of the backwards extrapolation technique applied to all of the experimental mea-

expected given that there is no dead time in the simulation.

1.7 Measurement Results

using 1000 replicates, discussed in Section 1.6.1. The table includes the dead time correction to the
benchmark quantities, as described in Section 1.6.2. Figure 31 shows the prompt-term compared to
the measurement results.

1.7.2 Efficiency Measurement – The efficiency measurement yielded a count rate of 1527.8 n/s,
as reported in Section 1.2.10.7. This measurement was performed with a well-characterized 252 Cf
neutron source, documented in Section 1.4.2. Performing the dead time correction as described in
Section 1.6.2 on the efficiency measurement results in a less than 1 n/s change in the measured count
rate. The decay corrected source strength is 745800 ± 10260 n/s, based on the calculations docu-
mented in Appendix C and a one-sigma uncertainty of ±1.376 % in the neutron emission rate41 . This
yields an overall detector efficiency of 0.205 ± 0.003 %. The efficiency of the experimental configu-
rations using the 7uPCX fuel rods is slightly different because of differences in source geometry. The
252 Cf neutron source is approximately a point source at the center of the tank while the fuel rod array

is a distributed cylindrical source with its edges closer to the detectors depending on the size of the
array. However, the neutron energy spectrum is expected to be similar since the neutrons are produced
from the spontaneous fission of 252 Cf and 250 Cf (see Table 28). The efficiency measurement is used
to adjust the active volume radius in the 3 He detector models and is evaluated in Section 2.2.9.

 41 The one-sigma uncertainty is based on the manufacturer reported 99% confidence interval of ±3.2 %, assuming a

normal distribution (i.e., ±3.2 % / 2.326 = ±1.376 %).

2.0 EVALUATION OF EXPERIMENTAL DATA

Each configuration was evaluated using MCNP® 6.3.0 with ENDF/B-VIII.0 neutron cross section li-
braries42,43 , using the re-release of the NJOY2016 processed thermal scattering law library44 . These
calculations utilize the Particle Track Output (PTRAC) feature of MCNP® 6.3.0 with the HDF5 out-
put file to produce the simulated time series neutron event data. The simulated duration of each model
is 65 min (3900 s). The first 5 min (300 s) of each simulation is truncated, resulting in a final simu-
lated duration of 60 min (3600 s). This duration was chosen to match the experimental measurements
and results in similar statistical uncertainties for Y sat and αp as reported in Table 33 of Section 1.7.
The neutron source is present in the UO2 fuel and uniformly sampled between 0 s and 3900 s, with
characteristics as described in Section 3.4.

The truncation of the simulated data is necessary as the neutron chains are long-lived in these thermal
experimental configurations, each with a very high subcritical multiplication. In the simulations, no
neutron chains exist before time t = 0. Therefore, truncating the start of the simulated neutron count
data is necessary to allow those neutron chains to build in. The selection of 300 s was based on the
results shown in Figure 32. This figure shows a simulation for each case where all source neutrons
were started at time t = 0 and reports the simulated neutron count rate fraction and the cumulative
counts as a function of time since each neutron chain began at t = 0. Truncating the first 300 s builds
in approximately 99.0 % of the neutron counts for Case 1, the highest multiplication configuration.
However, the remaining neutron counts still reach the detectors after up to 800 s.

The effect of uncertainty in each measured parameter was determined by calculating the sensitivity
in the benchmark quantity to that parameter. For a given parameter i with a mean value x0,i and a
perturbation δ xi , the resulting sensitivity in the benchmark quantity y, such as Y sat or αp , is

                                   ∂y     y(x0,i + δ xi ) − y(x0,i − δ xi )
 = (9)
 ∂ xi 2δ xi

The effect of the perturbation in the benchmark quantity for parameter i is symmetric unless otherwise
stated. While Eq. 9 describes a symmetric two-sided perturbation about the mean value x0,i , these
calculations can also be performed as a one-sided perturbation by setting one of the values of δ xi to
zero or as an asymmetric perturbation using two different values for δ xi .

The standard uncertainty uy,i in the benchmark quantity is then calculated using

  
 ∂y
                                             uy,i = ui                                                (10)
 ∂ xi

where ui is the evaluated uncertainty in parameter i and the sensitivity in the benchmark quantity
∂ y/∂ xi is either calculated using Eq. 9 or inferred from the slope of a linear least-squares fit of three
or more perturbations δ xi .

When the sensitivity in the benchmark quantity to parameter i can be assumed to be independent for
multiple perturbations δ xi , Eq. 10 may be represented as

 √
  
 ∂y
                                           uy,i = ui N                                                (11)
 ∂ xi
 √
where ui N is the resulting sum in quadrature of N independent uncertainties ui in parameter i. This
form is useful when evaluating the uncertainty in a parameter i involving multiple parts of a similar
type, allowing N perturbations to be performed with a single calculation.

There are two types of negligibility used in this evaluation. The first is negligibility in the sensitivity
of the benchmark quantities to an uncertain parameter. This negligibility is based on the propagated
uncertainty of the difference in Eq. 9 or uncertainty in the slope of the linear least-squares fit. If that
difference is not statistically significant for a variation that is reasonably greater than the evaluated
uncertainty in the parameter, then the sensitivity in the benchmark quantity to that uncertain parameter
is considered negligible.

The second is negligibility in the standard uncertainty of the benchmark quantities. This negligibility
is based on whether the standard uncertainty in the benchmark quantities due to an uncertain parameter
is large enough to meaningfully change the sum in quadrature of all uncertainties in that benchmark
quantity. This second negligibility is case-dependent and reported in Table 34.

2.1 Mass Uncertainty

The following sections evaluate the uncertainty in the benchmark quantities due to uncertainties in
mass. These uncertainties include the fuel rod UO2 mass and the 3 He detector fill gas pressure. The
other mass uncertainties are judged to be insignificant and not evaluated, as is done in LEU-COMP-
THERM-078, Rev. 0 and FUND-LLNL-ALPHAN-HE3-MULT-001, Rev. 0.

2.1.1 Fuel Rod UO2 Mass45 – The UO2 fuel pellet mass in each fuel rod (2199 total) and con-
trol/safety element fueled section (23 total) was measured. The average fuel mass in the entire pop-
ulation of 2222 fuel rods and control/safety element fueled sections was 108.7165 g with a standard
deviation of 0.32 g, reported in Table 5 of Section 1.2.8. The fuel mass measurements were made
using an instrument with a repeatability of 0.01 g, linearity of 0.02 g, and readability of 0.01 g. The
uncertainty in the fuel mass is ±0.3209 g, the sum in quadrature of the standard deviation in the
average fuel mass and the systematic measurement uncertainties.

Calculations vary the UO2 mass by collectively perturbing the mass of each fuel rod in the experimen-
tal configurations by ±1 g. These perturbations were performed by adjusting the density of the UO2
in the fuel rods while maintaining a constant volume. Table 35 reports the adjustment to the intrinsic
neutron source due to these perturbations, calculated as described in Appendix B.

2.1.2  3 He Detector Fill Gas Pressure46 – The 3 He detector fill gas pressure was reported by the

manufacturer as 0.21 MPa (30 psia). However, no tolerance or uncertainty in the pressure was given.
The uncertainty is taken to be one half the last significant digit of the specification, 0.005 MPa
(0.725 psia), which is a conservative estimate based on previous conversations with the manufacturer.

The Ideal Gas Law47 can be used to determine the density of the gas such that

 pV
 n= (12)
 RT

where n is the number of moles, p is pressure, V is volume, R is the Ideal Gas Constant (8.314 J mol−1 K−1 ),
and T is temperature.

Using the Ideal Gas Law in Eq. 12, this uncertainty in pressure corresponds to an uncertainty in
density of ±6.33 × 10−6 g/cm3 for a volume of 43.104 cm3 (based on dimensions reported in Fig. 22
of Section 1.5.1), pressure of 0.21 MPa, and temperature of 293.15 K.

Calculations vary the 3 He detector fill gas pressure by perturbing the density of all four 3 He detectors
in the experimental configurations by ±0.05 MPa (±6.33 × 10−5 g/cm3 ). These perturbations were
performed by adjusting the density of the 3 He fill gas while maintaining a constant volume.

Table 38 and Table 39 summarize the 3 He detector fill gas pressure uncertainty calculation parameters
and sensitivity in Y sat and αp , respectively. The standard uncertainty in Y sat and αp is calculated using
Eq. 10 where ui is the evaluated uncertainty in the 3 He detector fill gas pressure of ±0.005 MPa. The
sensitivity to the 3 He detector fill gas pressure for Cases 2 and 6 is not statistically significant, even
with a perturbation 10-times larger than the evaluated uncertainty. Therefore, both the sensitivity and
uncertainty are judged to be negligible for all cases. Table 38 and Table 39 are reproduced alongside
the other evaluated uncertainties in Section 2.6.

2.2 Dimension Uncertainty

2.2.1 Fuel Rod Pitch48 – The uncertainty in the fuel rod pitch contributes to the uncertainty in the
ratio of fuel to moderator in the core. This uncertainty is related to the uncertainty in the placement of
the holes in the grid plates during fabrication, to the width of the nominal gap between the outside of
the fuel rods and the inside of the grid plate holes, to the uncertainty in the diameter of the holes in the
grid plates, to the uncertainty in the outside diameter of the fuel rods, and to the number of rows of fuel
rods in the core. The fabrication tolerance in the placement of each hole is that it be within ±0.005 in.
(±0.0127 cm) of its intended location relative to the center of the grid plate. The nominal gap between
the outside of the fuel rods and the inside of the grid plate holes is 0.00625 in. (0.015875 cm). The
tolerance on the diameter of the grid plate holes is one-sided (+0.005/ − 0.000 in.). Assuming that
the nominal value of hole diameter falls at the center of this range, the tolerance is then ±0.0025 √ in.
(±0.00635 cm).
 √ The uncertainty in the fuel rod outer diameter  for the fuel array is 0.000086/ N in.
(0.000218/ N cm) where N is the number of fuel rods in the experimental configuration, which is
negligible for all experimental configurations when summed in quadrature with the systematic uncer-
tainty in the outer diameter measurements of 0.000022 √ in. (0.000056 cm). Multiplying the system-
atic uncertainty in the outer diameter measurements by 3 results in an uncertainty of 0.000038 in.
(0.000097 cm).

The fuel rod pitch uncertainty is based on these four uncertainties: the tolerance on the grid plate
hole placement; the nominal gap between the fuel rods and grid plate holes; the tolerance on the
diameter of the grid plate holes; and the uncertainty in the fuel rod outer diameter. Summing these
four uncertainties in quadrature yields:
 p
                      0.01272 + 0.0158752 + 0.006352 + 0.0000972 = 0.02130 cm

 48 This section is based on Section 2.4.1, Fuel Rod Pitch, LEU-COMP-THERM-078, Rev. 0.

Revision: 0 Page 63 of 171

The tolerance in the fuel rod pitch is twice this value divided by [the diameter of the core divided by
the fuel rod pitch]. Note that this last factor reduces to one less than the number of fuel rods on a
chord across the core.

For Cases 1 through 4, there were 37 fuel rods on a chord across the core. For these cases, the diameter
of the core divided by the fuel rod pitch is 36. Using the above method, the tolerance in the fuel rod
pitch is:

                                       (2 · 0.02130 cm) /36 = 0.00118 cm

For Cases 5 and 6, there were 35 fuel rods on a chord across the core. For these cases, the diameter
of the core divided by the fuel rod pitch is 35. Using the above method, the tolerance in the fuel rod
pitch is:

                                       (2 · 0.02130 cm) /34 = 0.00125 cm

 √
Dividing the above tolerances by 3 gives the one-standard-deviation value for the uncertainty in the
fuel rod pitch. The values so obtained are 0.000683 cm for Cases 1 through 4 and 0.000723 cm for
Cases 5 and 6.

in Y sat and αp , respectively. Calculations vary the fuel rod pitch by ±0.002 cm in increments of
0.001 cm. The sensitivity is based on the slope of a linear least-squares fit of these variations. The
uncertainty in the sensitivity uses the standard error in the slope of the least-squares fit. Table 40 and
Table 41 are reproduced alongside the other evaluated uncertainties in Section 2.6.

2.2.2 Clad Outer Diameter49 – The outer diameter of the fuel rod clad tubes was measured for the
rods available for the experiments. The population average for the measurements was 0.249980 in.
(0.634948 cm) with a standard
 √ deviation of√0.000086 in. (0.000218 cm). The uncertainty in the
mean value is 0.000086/ N in. (0.000218/ N cm) where N is the number of fuel rods in the ex-
perimental configuration. This corresponds to 0.0000026 in. (0.0000067 cm) for Case 1 (N=1058)
and 0.0000028 in. (0.0000071 cm) for Case 6 (N=948). The systematic uncertainty in the measure-
ments was 0.000022 in. (0.000056 cm). The resolution of the instruments used was 0.000001 in.
(0.00000254 cm) and the repeatability was 0.000005 in. (0.0000127 cm). The random uncertainty
in the diameter measurements was 0.000030 in. (0.0000762 cm) and will be treated as a system-
atic uncertainty. The sum in quadrature of the systematic uncertainties (0.000022 in., 0.000001 in.,
0.000005 in., and 0.000030 in.) is 0.0000375 in. (0.0000954 cm). The uncertainty in the clad outer di-
ameter is the sum in quadrature of the uncertainty in the mean value and systematic uncertainty, which
is dominated by the systematic uncertainty and results in an uncertainty of 0.000030 in. (0.0000956 cm)
for all cases.

Calculations vary the fuel cladding outer diameter by collectively perturbing the outer diameter of
each fuel rod in the experimental configurations by ±0.00508 cm, in increments of 0.00254 cm. These
perturbations were performed by adjusting the density of the cladding to maintain constant mass.

Table 42 and Table 43 summarize the fuel cladding outer diameter uncertainty calculation parameters
and sensitivity in Y sat and αp , respectively. The sensitivity is based on the slope of a linear least-
squares fit of these variations. The uncertainty in the sensitivity uses the standard error in the slope of
the least-squares fit. Table 42 and Table 43 are reproduced alongside the other evaluated uncertainties
in Section 2.6. It should be noted that while there is a significant sensitivity to the fuel cladding outer
diameter, the many measurements and low systematic uncertainty in those measurements result in
relatively small uncertainty in the benchmark quantities.

2.2.3 Clad Inner Diameter50 – The mass of the assembled clad tube and lower end cap was mea-
sured for 100 samples during the fabrication of the fuel rods. The average mass was 13.824 g with a
standard deviation of 0.027 g. The volume of the lower end cap was calculated from the dimensions
given in the design drawings as 0.354 cm3 . Using the tolerances given on the drawing, the uncertainty
in the volume is 0.010 cm3 . Using a density for 3003 aluminum alloy of 2.73 g/cm3 , the calculated
mass of the lower end cap is 0.967 g with a one-standard-deviation uncertainty based on drawing
tolerances of 0.027 g. The mass of the 29.75 in. (75.565 cm) long clad tube is then 12.857 g with
an uncertainty of 0.027 g. The average measured outside diameter of the fuel rods is 0.249980 in.
(0.634948 cm) with standard deviation for 2194 measurements of 0.000086 in. (0.000218 cm) and an
overall uncertainty of 0.000023 in. (0.000058 cm) including systematic uncertainties. From these data
and using a density of 2.73 g/cm3 , an inner diameter of 0.569038 cm is obtained with an uncertainty
of 0.000164 cm.

2.2.4 Fuel Outer Diameter51 – The outer diameter of 123 randomly-selected fuel pellets was mea-
sured. The average diameter was 0.20694 in. (0.52563 cm) with a standard deviation of 0.00019 in.
(0.00048 cm). The systematic uncertainties in the fuel pellet outer diameter measurements are 0.000001 in.
resolution, 0.000005 in. repeatability, 0.000022 in. absolute uncertainty, and 0.000030 in. reproducibil-

the uncertainty in the average diameter is 0.000041 in. (0.000105 cm).

performed by adjusting the density of the UO2 fuel to maintain constant mass.

2.2.6 Fuel Rod Pellet Stack Height53 – The fuel pellet stack height was measured during fabri-
cation for all fuel rods to the nearest millimeter. The systematic uncertainty in this measurement is
estimated to be 0.05 cm. The standard deviation for 2222 fuel columns was 0.125 cm. The uncertainty
in the average fuel pellet stack height is the estimated systematic uncertainty in the measurement or
0.05 cm. The uncertainty in the average fuel pellet stack height is the sum in quadrature of the uncer-
tainty in the mean value and systematic uncertainty which is 0.135 cm.

Calculations vary the UO2 fuel height in the fuel rods by ±1.0 cm with an opposite variation in the
spring height. These variations were performed by adjusting the densities of the UO2 fuel and springs
to maintain constant mass.

2.2.9 Detector Active Volume – The detector active volume represents the volume in which the
3 He(n,p) reaction is tallied in the model. The manufacturer reports a nominal active length of 36 in.

(91.4 cm). The radius of the active volume is affected by the detector system and the wall effect54 .
The detector efficiency measurement, described in Sections 1.2.10.7 and 1.7.2, was performed to
determine the active volume radius.

The detector efficiency measurement used the 252 Cf spontaneous fission neutron source described in
Section 1.4.2. The neutron source was attached to the fixture described in Section 1.4.2.1 and placed
in the central location of the grid plate, with the bottom of the source fixture 24.31796 cm above the
top of the lower grid plate. The control/safety elements were fully withdrawn to their most reactive
positions, leaving the twelve fueled follower sections in the core. Figure 33 shows the model used
 54 G. F. Knoll. Radiation Detection and Measurement. 4th ed. John Wiley & Sons, 2010. Refer to Ch. 14, Section II.A,

for a general description of the wall effect and Ch. 14, Section III.B, for the wall effect in 3 He proportional counters.

Revision: 0 Page 71 of 171

to perform the detector efficiency measurement calculation. The simulations were performed in the
same manner as described in Section 2.0, except that only the neutron count rate is derived from the
simulated time series neutron data.

have a negligible impact and are not evaluated.
