2.3 Position Uncertainty

2.3.1 3 He Detector Position – The outer diameter of the 3 He detectors is 5.08 cm, described in
Section 1.5.1, and the inner diameter of the detector dry wells is 5.6388 cm, described in Section 1.2.4.
This allows for a tolerance on the position of the detector within the dry√ well of ±0.2794 cm. Since
this represents a tolerance, the uncertainty in the position is 0.2794/ 3 cm. This uncertainty also
accounts for any off-axis tilt in the detectors.

Table 60 and Table 61 summarize the 3 He detector position uncertainty calculation parameters and
sensitivity in Y sat and αp , respectively. Calculations vary the 3 He detector position by ±0.2794 cm
with respect to the axial center of each detector dry well (along the x-axis for Channels 2 and 4 and
along the y-axis for Channels 1 and 3). The standard uncertainty in Y sat and αp is calculated
 √ using
Eq. 10 where ui is the evaluated uncertainty in the 3 He detector position of 0.2794/ 3 cm. The
sensitivity to the 3 He detector position for Cases 2 and 6 is not statistically significant, even for a
perturbation representing the physical bounds of the parameter. Therefore, both the sensitivity and
uncertainty are judged to be negligible for all cases. Table 60 and Table 61 are reproduced alongside
the other evaluated uncertainties in Section 2.6.

2.3.2 Dry Well Position – The outer diameter of the detector dry wells is 6.35 cm, described in
Section 1.2.4. The dry wells were positioned in the drainage holes of the lower grid plate which have
a diameter of 10.16 cm. During the experiment the dry wells were pushed to the side of the drainage
holes nearest to the fuel assembly, as shown in Figure 23 of Section 1.5.2. While positioning the dry
wells within the tank, care was taken to ensure the dry well tubes were axially aligned with respect to
the fuel assembly grid plates, as shown in Figure 9 of Section 1.2.4. However, positioning these long
tubes within the assembly tank was not exact.

Table 62 reports the cartesian coordinates of the measured dry well positions, based on the measure-
ments reported in Table 31 of Section 1.5.2, and nominal positions, where the dry wells were intended
to be placed during the experiment. Figure 34 shows the dry well positions in Table 31, including the
drainage holes and the upper grid plate. The measurements for the Channel 3 and 4 detectors imply
that the dry wells "overlap" the drainage hole. While this is not physical, it is likely due to the detector,
which the distance-to-center measurements are relative to, not being centered in the dry well or to the
dry well not being axially aligned with respect to the fuel assembly grid plates. It could also be due
to uncertainty in the measurements which were performed by hand using a tape measure. Therefore,
a bounding value on the one-sigma-uncertainty in the dry well positions is estimated to be ±1.0 cm.
This value also accounts for any off-axis tilt in the dry wells.

2.4 Material Uncertainty

2.4.1 Fuel Enrichment55 – The fuel isotopics were measured on ten randomly-selected fuel pel-
let samples drawn from the pellet stock used in the experiment fuel rods, reported in Table 17 of
Section 1.3.1. The standard deviation of the 235 U enrichment measurements was 0.0046 wt.%. The
systematic uncertainty was 0.0069 wt.%. Considering the random uncertainty and the number of
measurements and adding in quadrature to the systematic uncertainty, the uncertainty in the 235 U
enrichment of the fuel is 0.0071 wt.%.

Calculations vary the UO2 enrichment by ±0.2 wt.% 235 U. The UO2 fuel mass and volume were held
constant with changes in the 235 U mass fraction compensated by changes in the 238 U mass fraction.
Table 65 reports the adjustment to the intrinsic neutron source due to these perturbations, calculated
as described in Appendix B.

2.4.4 Fuel Stoichiometry58 – The oxygen-to-uranium ratio in the fuel was not measured and is
assumed to be 2.0. A range of 0.01 is assumed to bound the one-sigma uncertainty in the oxygen-to-
uranium ratio59 .

Calculations vary the oxygen-to-uranium ratio by ±0.2 (i.e., UO1.8 to UO2.2 ) in increments of 0.1.
These perturbations were performed by adjusting the density of the UO2 fuel to maintain a constant
mass. Table 74 reports the adjustment to the intrinsic neutron source due to these perturbations,
calculated as described in Appendix B.

2.4.5 Impurities in the UO2 Fuel60 – The impurities in the UO2 fuel are reported in Table 18 of
Section 1.3.1. These impurities fell into two classes - those for which a definite value was measured
and those that were determined to be less than the detection limit for the analysis system. For this
analysis, an "impurity unit" (i.u.) will be defined as the level of impurities detected or the detection
limit for those impurities not detected. For the impurities that were detected, an uncertainty at the
one-standard-deviation level of 50 % of the detected value (0.5 i.u.) was assumed. These impurities
include: Ag, B, Cd, Co, Cr, Cu, Fe, Mn, Mo, Ni, V, and W. For the impurities that were below the
detection limit, an uncertainty at the one-standard-deviation level of the detected limit was assumed.
These impurities include: Sm, Dy, Eu, and Gd.

Calculations vary the UO2 fuel impurity content between none of the impurities (0 i.u.), the reported
level (1 i.u.), and twice the reported level with all impurities (2 i.u.). The fuel mass and volume were
 60 This section is based on Section 2.4.12, Impurities in the UO Fuel, LEU-COMP-THERM-078, Rev. 0.
 2

Revision: 0 Page 83 of 171

held constant during the variations as were the uranium isotopics and oxygen-to-uranium atom ratio.
The UO2 fuel composition perturbations do not have a noticeable change to the intrinsic neutron
source.

Table 77 and Table 78 summarize the UO2 fuel impurity uncertainty calculation parameters and sen-
sitivity in Y sat and αp , respectively. The sensitivity is based on the slope of a linear least-squares
fit of these variations. The uncertainty in the sensitivity uses the standard error in the slope of the
least-squares fit. The sensitivity to impurities in the UO2 fuel for Cases 2 and 6 is not statistically
significant, even with a perturbation of twice the evaluated uncertainty. Therefore, both the sensitiv-
ity and uncertainty are judged to be negligible for all cases. Table 77 and Table 78 are reproduced
alongside the other evaluated uncertainties in Section 2.6.

2.4.6 Fuel Clad Composition61 – The clad tubes and end caps for the fuel rods were fabricated from
3003 aluminum alloy. The elemental composition of the 3003 aluminum alloy was not measured. The
composition range for 3003 aluminum alloy tubing is reported in Table 19 of Section 1.3.2. For the
work documented here, the composition of the tubes and end caps is assumed to be at the mid-range
value where an elemental content is specified as a range and as half of the maximum value where one
is given for an element. The density of the 3003 aluminum alloy was taken as 2.73 g/cm3 .

The effect of changes in the concentration of any given element depends on the absorption cross
section of the element relative to that of aluminum. Table 79 lists the thermal neutron absorption cross
section for each element listed in the composition of 3003 aluminum alloy. Most of the elements
listed have a larger absorption cross section than aluminum. The exception is Si. The maximum
absorption cross section for 3003 aluminum alloy occurs for the composition that has the maximum

 61 This section is based on Section 2.1.3, Fuel Rod Cladding Composition, and Section 2.4.13, Fuel Clad Composition,

of LEU-COMP-THERM-078, Rev. 0.

Revision: 0 Page 84 of 171

concentration of all elements except Si. The content of Si is minimized, replaced by aluminum, to
obtain the maximum absorption cross section.

 Table 79. Thermal neutron absorption cross sections for all elements listed in the composition for
 3003 aluminum alloy.

 Element σa (barn)(a) Element σa (barn)(a)
                              Al            0.230            Cu              3.8
                              Si            0.168            Mn             13.3
                              Fe             2.56            Zn              1.1
 (a) Thermal neutron (2200 m/s) absorption cross section from J.

 R. Parrington, et al., Nuclides and Isotopes Fifteenth Edition,
 General Electric, Co., 1996.

Calculations included 3003 aluminum alloy containing the nominal composition (0 c.u.), the mini-
mum absorption composition (-1 c.u.), the maximum absorption composition (1 c.u.), and twice the
maximum absorption composition (2 c.u.). The nominal composition is described above. The mini-
mum absorption composition was formulated by including all elements at the minimum concentration
except Si, which was included at the maximum concentration. The maximum absorption composition
was formulated inversely with all elements included at maximum concentration except Si, which was
included at the minimum concentration.

The change from one composition to the nominal composition can be defined as one “composition
unit” (c.u.). In this case, the composition unit is the change of the composition by half the range for
elements given as a range of values and half of the maximum for elements with maximum content
given, all elements being changed in concert. Because the composition is√specified as limits, the
one-standard-deviation variation of the composition is assumed to be ±1.0/ 3 c.u..

Table 80 and Table 81 summarize the 3003 aluminum alloy cladding composition uncertainty calcu-
lation parameters and sensitivity in Y sat and αp , respectively. Calculations vary the 3003 aluminum
alloy cladding composition from -1 c.u. to 2 c.u. in increments of 1 c.u.. The sensitivity is based on
the slope of a linear least-squares fit of these variations. The uncertainty in the sensitivity uses the
standard error in the slope of the least-squares fit. Table 80 and Table 81 are reproduced alongside the
other evaluated uncertainties in Section 2.6.

2.4.7 Aluminum Grid Plate Composition62 – The elemental composition of the 6061 aluminum
alloy grid plates was measured and reported by the supplier of the grid plates. No uncertainties were
given for the measurements. The measured composition for the aluminum grid plates is shown in
Table 20 of Section 1.3.3. The dry wells were also made of 6061 aluminum alloy.

The 6061 aluminum alloy grid plates were treated in a manner similar to the 3003 aluminum alloy
cladding. Table 82 lists the thermal neutron absorption cross sections for the measured constituents of
the aluminum. All of the listed elements have larger absorption cross sections than aluminum except
Si and Mg.

Calculations included 6061 aluminum alloy containing the nominal composition (0 c.u.), a minimum
absorption composition (-1 c.u.), and a maximum absorption composition (1 c.u.). For this analysis,
the bounding composition for the minimum absorption had the content of all constituents reduced
50 % from the measured value except Si and Mg, which were increased by 50 %. The bounding
composition for the maximum absorption had the reverse - all elements increased by 50 % except Si
and Mg, which were reduced 50 %.

The change from one composition to the nominal composition can be defined as one “composition
unit” (c.u.). In this case, the composition unit is the change of the constituents by 100 % of the
measured value. A somewhat arbitrary but bounding assumption was made that the uncertainty in the
measured composition of the aluminum was ±0.25 c.u..

Table 83 and Table 84 summarize the 6061 aluminum alloy grid plate composition uncertainty calcu-
lation parameters and sensitivity in Y sat and αp , respectively. Calculations vary the 6061 aluminum
alloy grid plate composition from -1 c.u. to 1 c.u. in increments of 1 c.u.. The sensitivity is based
on the slope of a linear least-squares fit of these variations. The uncertainty in the sensitivity uses
the standard error in the slope of the least-squares fit. The sensitivity to the 6061 aluminum alloy
grid plate composition for Cases 2 and 6 is not statistically significant, even with a perturbation 4-
times larger than the evaluated uncertainty. Therefore, both the sensitivity and uncertainty are judged
to be negligible for all cases. Table 83 and Table 84 are reproduced alongside the other evaluated
uncertainties in Section 2.6.

composition from 0 i.u. to 10 i.u. in increments of 5 i.u. of the reported maximum impurities. The
city-wide maximum√is assumed to represent a tolerance. Therefore, the uncertainty in the water
composition is ±1/ 12 i.u..

Table 85 and Table 86 summarize the water composition uncertainty calculation parameters and sen-
sitivity in Y sat and αp , respectively. The sensitivity is based on the slope of a linear least-squares fit
of the above variations. The uncertainty in the sensitivity uses the standard error in the slope of the
least-squares fit. The uncertainty in Y sat and αp is negligible for Cases 2 and 6. Therefore, the un-
certainty is judged to be negligible for all cases. Table 85 and Table 86 are reproduced alongside the

2.5 Temperature Uncertainty

The temperature of the water moderator was measured using thermocouples at three different axial
levels in the reflector. The average temperature for each thermocouple was determined by averaging
the thermocouple readings over the time of the measurements, reported in Section 1.3.5.1. The exper-
iments were run near a temperature of 25 ◦ C. A bounding estimate of the uncertainty in the measured
experiment temperature is ±2.5 ◦ C.

The relevant ACE-formatted thermal scattering laws based on ENDF/B-VIII.0 for water distributed
with MCNP® include the following temperatures (with ACE library identifier)64 : 293.6 K (40t), 284 K
(41t), and 300 K (42t). The water thermal scattering law is specific to the 1 H neutron cross section for
elastic scattering.

Table 87 reports the temperatures of the three water thermal scattering laws evaluated as part of this
uncertainty along with the corresponding water density. In this analysis, only the water density of the
water and thermal scattering law (1 H in H2 O) are adjusted. All other temperature effects are assumed
to be negligible.

 Table 87. Density and thermal scattering law temperatures evaluated for water.

 Water Density
 Temperature (K) Temperature (°C)
                                                                               (g/cm3 )(a)
                                284.0                     10.9                   0.99962
                                293.6                     20.5                   0.99811
                                300.0                     26.9                   0.99656
 (a) National Institute of Standards and Technology Chemistry Web-

 Book: http://webbook.nist.gov/chemistry/ (Models and
 Tools > Thermophysical Properties of Fluid Systems).

Table 88 and Table 89 summarize the temperature uncertainty calculation parameters and sensitivity
in Y sat and αp , respectively. Calculations model the temperature of the water moderator at 284 K,
293.6 K, and 300 K. The sensitivity is based on the slope of a linear least-squares fit of these vari-
ations. The uncertainty in the sensitivity uses the standard error in the slope of the least-squares fit.
Table 88 and Table 89 are reproduced alongside the other evaluated uncertainties in Section 2.6.

2.6 Combined Uncertainty

The results of the sensitivity studies were combined with the uncertainties in the various parameters
to determine the contribution of each uncertainty source to the overall uncertainty in the experiments.
The results for the prompt asymptotic value (Y sat ) and prompt decay constant (αp ) are reported in
Tables 91 and 92 for Case 1, in Tables 93 and 94 for Case 2, in Tables 95 and 96 for Case 3, in
Tables 97 and 98 for Case 4, in Tables 99 and 100 for Case 5, and in Tables 101 and 102 for Case 6.
The tables include the total uncertainty for each benchmark quantity which represents the sum in
quadrature of all parameters. The largest sources of uncertainty are the fuel rod pitch and the fuel rod
cladding composition for both Y sat and αp , as well as the detector dry well position for Y sat .

Revision: 0 Page 103 of 171

3.0 BENCHMARK SPECIFICATIONS65

The benchmark models are a combination of the fuel tank and array model from LEU-COMP-
THERM-078, Rev. 0 and the 3 He detector model from FUND-LLNL-ALPHAN-HE3-MULT-001,
Rev. 0. In addition to the model description for this evaluation, the differences and additions between
these previous models are noted throughout this section.

The following modeling approximations from LEU-COMP-THERM-078, Rev. 0 have been made
and judged to have an insignificant effect on the benchmark quantities:

 • Nominal dimensions of the fuel rods and average mass based on the entire population of fuel
 rods.
 • Alignment of aluminum spacers within the fuel rods to the axial position of the upper grid plate.
 • Removal of experiment parts outside of the reflector. This includes the experiment parts above
 the reflector, the tank wall around the reflector, and the experiment room.
 • Removal of upper grid plate support bosses and posts. These include the four aluminum posts
 that attach to support bosses at the corners of the upper grid plate and guide plate.
 • Simplify control and safety elements as fuel rods. The fueled sections and polyethylene decou-
 pler sections of the control and safety elements were designed to be similar to the design of the
 fuel rods.

3.1 Description of Model

The benchmark model includes the assembly tank with water, upper and lower grid plates, fuel rods,
Plant Protection System (PPS) dry wells and polyethylene, and the four 3 He detectors in dry wells.
Each configuration, including the 3 He detector positions, is identical except for the number and loca-
tions of the fuel rods. Figure 35 shows a schematic of the benchmark model.

 65 The initial benchmark model geometry and description was developed by Eric Hudec (Oregon State University,

USA) under the advisement of Todd Palmer (Oregon State University, USA) and Daniel Siefman (University of California,
Berkeley, and Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory, USA).

Revision: 0 Page 104 of 171

 Figure 35. Schematic of the benchmark model, showing the horizontal (top) and vertical (bottom)
 cross sections.

3.2 Dimensions

3.2.1 Assembly Tank and Fuel Rods66 – The assembly tank and fuel rods can be modeled as
a cylinder of water with two grid plates in it supporting a 45x45 square-pitched array of fuel rod
positions centered on the axis of the cylinder. Not all of the array positions are fueled - some have no
fuel rod. A cut-away perspective view of the benchmark model is shown in Figure 36. A layout of
the benchmark model is shown in Figure 37.

 66 This section is based on Section 3.2, Dimensions, of LEU-COMP-THERM-078, Rev. 0.

Revision: 0 Page 105 of 171

Figure 36. Cut-away perspective view of the benchmark model, excluding the 3 He detectors and dry
 wells and the six drainage holes in the lower grid plate. Dimensions in cm.

Revision: 0 Page 106 of 171

Figure 37. Composite cross section of the benchmark model, showing the fuel rods in the upper grid
 plate and the drainage holes in the lower grid plate without the 3 He detector dry wells. Dimensions
 in cm.

The water moderator and reflector is a right circular cylinder 93.6752 cm in diameter and 87.3252 cm
tall. The lower grid plate is a cylinder 92.71 cm diameter and 2.54 cm thick centered on the axis of
the moderator cylinder. The lower grid plate has six 10.16 cm diameter drainage holes through it67 .
The top of the lower grid plate is 19.05 cm above the bottom of the water cylinder. The lower grid
plate has a 45x45 square-pitched array of blind cylindrical holes bored from the top surface 1.27 cm
deep that support the fuel rods from the bottom. The design drawings specify a minimum diameter
of 0.6604 cm for the holes that position the fuel rods in the fuel array with a one-sided tolerance of
0.0127 cm. The grid plate hole diameter in the benchmark model is set at the center of the tolerance
band at 0.66675 cm (0.333375 cm radius). The upper grid plate is a 41.91 cm square 2.54 cm thick
centered on the axis of the water cylinder. The bottom of the upper grid plate is 50.4952 cm above
the top surface of the lower grid plate. The upper grid plate has a 45x45 square-pitched array of
0.333375 cm radius through holes bored in it to locate the fuel rods in the array. The pitch of the fuel
rod array is 0.854964 cm. The vertical axis of the central fuel rod position in the array is positioned
at the center of the two grid plates and therefore on the vertical axis of the moderator cylinder.

The fuel rods in the model extend from the bottom of the holes in the lower grid plate to the surface
of the water68 . In the description that follows, the upper surface of the lower grid plate, also the axial
location of the bottom of the fuel in the fuel rods, is the origin of the axial coordinates. The fuel rods

 67 The six drainage holes are not included in the LEU-COMP-THERM-078 benchmark model.
 68 LEU-COMP-THERM-078 includes a description of aluminum rods which are not present in this experiment.

Revision: 0 Page 107 of 171

are right-circular cylinders. Figure 38 shows a schematic of several fuel rods and an empty cell in the
model. Table 103 lists modeling information by axial interval for the fuel rods. Table 104 lists similar
information for array positions that are unfueled.

3.3.3 Dry Wells – The detector dry wells are 6061 aluminum alloy, modeled using the same material

3.4 Source Strength and Spectra

There was no external neutron source present during the benchmark measurements. Instead, the
neutron source is due to the intrinsic source of the UO2 fuel, produced from spontaneous fission (SF)
and (α,n) reactions. The composition of the UO2 fuel is reported in Table 109. SOURCES 4C72
is used to calculate the spontaneous fission and (α,n) neutron emission rates and spectra assuming a
perfectly homogeneous mixture. The SOURCES 4C input and results are documented in Appendix B.

 72 W. B. Wilson et al. SOURCES 4C: A Code for Calculating (alpha,n), Spontaneous Fission, and Delayed Neutron

Sources and Spectra. LA-UR-02-1839. Los Alamos National Laboratory, 2002. URL: https : / / www . osti . gov /
biblio/976142.

Revision: 0 Page 128 of 171

Table 120 reports the neutron source rate of the UO2 fuel. The spontaneous fission neutron source
is dominated by 238 U while the 234 U(α,n)18 O reaction accounts for the majority of (α,n) neutrons.
Table 125 and Table 126 of Appendix B report the (α,n) and spontaneous fission neutron source rates
for each relevant nuclide in the UO2 fuel. Figure 50 shows the (α,n) and spontaneous fission neutron
source spectra with 100 energy bins from 0 MeV to 10 MeV. Tabulated data for the neutron source
spectra are included in Table 127 of Appendix B.

 APPENDIX C: 252 CF NEUTRON SOURCE DECAY CORRECTION

This appendix documents the source strength and spectrum of the 252 Cf neutron source used to per-
form the detector efficiency measurement on July 22, 2022. Due to the source being manufactured
in 2018, the californium composition and resulting neutron source strength must be decay corrected
to the date of the efficiency measurement. The decay calculation is performed using FISPACT-II76
and verified analytically. The neutron source emission rate is calculated using SOURCES 4C77 with
the result of the decay calculation as the composition and updated decay data for the californium iso-
topes. The source spectrum is given by the 252 Cf spontaneous fission (SF) neutron spectrum from the
Version II of the International Reactor Dosimetry and Fusion File (IRDFF-II)78 .

C.1 Decay Corrected Composition

The exponential form of the radioactive decay law is

                                                 A(t) = A0 e−λt                                               (13)

where A(t) is the activity at time t, A0 is the initial activity, and λ is the decay constant. Given the
activity of a nuclide, its mass can be determined using

 A(t) NA · λ
                                          m(t) =          ,   a=                                              (14)
 a M

where m(t) and A(t) are the mass and activity at time t, a is the specific activity, M is the atomic
mass, NA is Avogadro’s number, and λ is the decay constant. Table 128 summarizes the decay data
for californium used to calculate the activity and mass analytically.

 76 G. Bailey et al. The FISPACT-II User Manual. UKAEA-CCFE-RE(21)02. UK Atomic Energy Authority, 2023.
URL : https://fispact.ukaea.uk/documentation-2/manual/.
 77 W. B. Wilson et al. SOURCES 4C: A Code for Calculating (alpha,n), Spontaneous Fission, and Delayed Neutron

Sources and Spectra. LA-UR-02-1839. Los Alamos National Laboratory, 2002. URL: https : / / www . osti . gov /
biblio/976142.
 78 A. Trkov et al. “IRDFF-II: A New Neutron Metrology Library”. Special Issue of Nuclear Data Sheets 163 (2020),

pp. 1–108. DOI: 10.1016/j.nds.2019.12.001.

Revision: 0 Page 140 of 171

 Table 128. Decay data for californium isotopes.

 Atomic Half-Life Decay Constant, Specific Activity,
 Nuclide
 Mass, M (a) Years(b) Seconds λ (1/s) a (Bq/g)(c)
       249 Cf          249.0748        350.7       1.1060E+10      6.2673E-11        1.5153E+11
       250 Cf          250.0764        13.08       4.1249E+08      1.6804E-09        4.0466E+12
       251 Cf          251.0796         898        2.8319E+10      2.4476E-11        5.8706E+10
       252 Cf          252.0816        2.647       8.3476E+07      8.3036E-09        1.9837E+13
 (a) M. Wang et al. The AME2020 Atomic Mass Evaluation. 2021. DOI : 10.1088/1674-1137/abddaf.
 (b) E. A. McCutchan et al. Nuclear Wallet Cards, 9th Edition.
 National Nuclear Data Center, Brookhaven
 National Laboratory, Oct. 2023. URL: https://www.nndc.bnl.gov/walletcards/.
 (c) Calculated using Eq. 14, where N = 6.0221 × 1023 .
 A

Table 129 reports the decay corrected californium composition of the 252 Cf neutron source. The
certification of calibration for the source reported the californium composition on Jan. 30, 2018 and
the activity (total and 252 Cf only) on Apr. 15, 2018. The composition on Jan. 30, 2018 was decayed
to Apr. 15, 2018 using Eq. 13 then scaled to the reported total activity of 20190 kBq on Apr. 15, 2018,
giving the initial activity on Jan. 30, 2018. Then, this initial activity was decayed to the experiment
date on Jul. 22, 2022.

The analytic calculation and FISPACT-II results are in good agreement. The minor discrepancies in
the activity and mass of the two calculations are due to the differences in the nuclear constants used
in the calculations. However, these differences are insignificant compared to reported uncertainty on
the certificate of calibration (3.2 % at the 99 % confidence level).

Additionally, the FISPACT-II calculation included the 248 Cm and 246 Cm daughter products which
also undergo SF. However, at this timescale, the contribution to the neutron emission rate due to these
daughter products is negligible.

 APPENDIX D: NUMERICAL ANALYSIS OF FIT AND CONVERGENCE

As shown and described in Section 1.6, the Feynman-alpha (variance-to-mean) method requires a
two-term non-linear fit in order to accurately represent these experimental measurements. For the
parameters of this fit to be useful as benchmark quantities, the fit must be shown as numerically
converged. To do so, this appendix summarizes the analysis used to show that the selected range of
time gates (lower and upper) and the number of time gates are fully converged.

D.1 Time Gate Range

To determine the range of time gates necessary for convergence, an upper and lower sweep was
performed. The upper sweep involved fixing the upper time gate at either 10−1 s, 1 s, or 101 s, then
sweeping the lower time gates from 10−2 s to 10−5 s. The lower sweep involved fixing the lower
time gate at either 10−5 s, 10−4 s, or 10−3 s, then sweeping the upper time gates from 10−2 s to 101 s.
Figure 51 shows a visualization of how these time gate sweeps were performed, overlaid on the
variance-to-mean curve of the Case 6 experimental measurement.

 Figure 51. Example time gate sweeping of the Case 6 experimental measurement.

The following figures show the results of the upper and lower time gate sweeps for the experimental
measurements, in order of increasing neutron multiplication (Case 6 through 1). These plots present
the resulting fit parameters corresponding to Y sat and αp as defined in Eq. 7 of Section 1.6. The top
two plots of each figure show the lower time gate sweeps for Y sat (top-left) and αp (top-right) while
the bottom two plots show the upper time gate sweeps for Y sat (bottom-left) and αp (bottom-right).
Each plot shows the benchmark quantity and uncertainty, as reported in Table 33 of Section 1.7, using
the shaded region. The purpose of this shaded region is to show the range of time gates where the
result is statistically the same as the reported benchmark quantities.

D.2 Number of Time Gates

To determine the number of time gates necessary for convergence, the number of time gates was
increased from 6 to 51 on a time gate range84 of 10−4 s to 101 s. These numbers of time gates represent
from 1 to 10 time gates per decade of the time gate range. Within the time range, the time gates were
sampled uniformly spaced in logarithmic time. Each number of time gates was bootstrapped, as
described in Section 1.6.1, to determine the mean and standard deviation of the resulting Y sat and αp .

The following figures show the results of the increasing number of time gates and resulting bootstrap
uncertainty in Y sat and αp , in order of increasing neutron multiplication (Case 6 through 1). These plots
present the resulting fit parameters corresponding to Y sat and αp as defined in Eq. 7 of Section 1.6.
The top two plots of each figure show Y sat (top-left) and its relative bootstrap uncertainty (top-right)
while the bottom two plots show αp (bottom-left) and its relative bootstrap uncertainty (bottom-right).

 Figure 58. Increasing number of time gates for Case 6, the lowest multiplication configuration.

 84 This time gate range is slightly different than the 10−4 s to 1 s range used to calculate the experimental measurement

results in Table 33 of Section 1.7.

Revision: 0 Page 154 of 171

D.3 Conclusion and Recommended Fit Parameters

The upper time gates begin to converge between 10−1 s and 1 s while the lower time gates converge
below 1 × 10−4 s. As the upper time gate increases beyond 1 s, both Y sat and αp begin to diverge, due
to the larger time gates having large uncertainty. This can also be seen in the lower time gates, which
all converge to slightly different values based on their corresponding fixed upper time gate. Therefore,
a time gate range of 10−4 s to 1 s is necessary for these experimental measurements.

The number of time gates converges around 5-per-decade. Using less than that results in the uncer-
tainty in Y sat and αp being underestimated for the higher multiplication configurations (Cases 1-4).
Therefore, a total of 29 time gates, representing 7-per-decade for the time gate range of 10−4 s to 1 s,
will be used for these experimental measurements. These recommended time widths and correspond-
ing number of bunchings are reported in Table 132.

Revision: 0 Page 160 of 171

  APPENDIX E: DEAD TIME CORRECTION OF THE BENCHMARK MEASUREMENTS

As described in Section 1.6.2, the backwards extrapolation technique is applied to the experimental
measurements to correct for losses due to dead time. All figures in this appendix show a first order
polynomial fit of the backwards extrapolation technique on the average count rate (left plots), prompt
asymptotic value (middle plots), and prompt decay constant (right plots). This fit is limited to dead
times ≥4 µs, shown as the blue points circled in black.

Figure 64 and Figure 65 show the backwards extrapolation technique applied to the Case 6 experi-
mental measurement and the Case 6 benchmark simulation, respectively. The backwards extrapolation
technique applied to this simulation is performed exactly as described in Section 1.6.2, specifically
only dead times ≥4 µs are used in the fit of the first order polynomial. Since the benchmark simulation
effectively has zero dead time85 , this comparison serves as a qualitative validation of this technique
to correct the experimental measurements in this evaluation.

 APPENDIX F: INDEPENDENT REVIEW

The following appendix was contributed by Belen Monteagudo Godoy of the Autorité de Sûreté
Nucléaire et de Radioprotection (ASNR) with results reported in Section 4.0.

Revision: 0 Page 165 of 171

 1 ASNR Review - MORET 6
 ASNR conducted an independent external review based on the simplified benchmark model described
 in Section 3. A comparison of the experimental data with simulations using ASNR’s Monte Carlo
 code MORET 6 [1] is summarized in this Appendix.

 1.1 Analysis of experimental data from LLNL
 The raw experimental data were provided by LLNL in LMX format and were independently ana-
 lyzed by ASNR for all configurations. The Feynman Variance-to-Mean, Y(τ ), was calculated using
 the Hage-Cifarelli formalism, following the same procedure described in Section 1.6. In this ap-
 proach, time-tagged neutron events are divided into gates of equal width τ , typically on the order
 of a few microseconds, and the number of events within each gate is counted. This process is re-
 peated across several time widths, with each gate corresponding to a point of the Y(τ ) curve. If
 the gate width is not an integer multiple of the measurement time, the remaining data are discarded.

 The uncertainty in the Feynman-alpha method was estimated through error propagation (see
 Ref. [2]), instead of using the Bootstrap method for a time-series analysis as described in Section
 1.6.1. To determine the benchmark quantities (the asymptotic value, Ysat,p , and the decay constant,
 αp , of the prompt neutron term) Y(τ ) was fitted to Equation 7 using a non-linear least squares
 fitting algorithm (Levenberg-Marquardt). The uncertainties in Ysat,p and αp were calculated as the
 standard errors derived from the covariance matrix of the fit. A comparison between the fitting
 results of ASNR’s approach and those of LLNL’s approach is presented in Table 1. Both, Ysat,p and
 αp , show strong agreement, with differences of no more than 1 or 2 sigma.

 Ysat αp [1/s]
 Configuration ASNR LLNL zscore ASNR LLNL zscore
              1         121.14 ± 0.21    124.00 ± 4.44        0.64    296.1 ± 0.9   279.9 ± 24.4    0.66
              2         112.22 ± 0.22    114.85 ± 3.64        0.72    311.4 ±1.0    295.7 ± 22.6    0.69
              3          81.11 ± 0.21     81.68 ± 1.46        0.39    366.5 ± 1.7   364.1 ±15.3     0.15
              4          48.84 ± 0.17     48.98 ± 0.65        0.21      454 ± 3     453.2 ± 14.4    0.06
              5          23.08 ± 0.11     23.01 ± 0.18        0.32      637 ± 6     653.4 ± 12.3    1.22
              6           8.47 ± 0.06      8.41 ± 0.05        0.74     945 ± 16     985.1 ± 14.3    1.85

 Table 1: Experimental benchmark quantities, Ysat,p and αp , obtained from the independent ASNR
 and LLNL analyses

 1.2 MORET 6 simulations
 Both criticality and analog simulations were performed by ASNR using MORET 6, based on the
 geometry of the benchmark model described in Section 3. MORET 6 is the in-house Monte Carlo
 code developed by ASNR, specifically designed for fixed source, microscopic time-dependent and
 fission simulations.

 Criticality Approach

 Criticality calculations were performed as a preliminary step before running the more time-
 consuming analog simulations. The main goal was to compare the kef f results between MNCP 6.3.0

 1

Revision: 0 Page 166 of 171

 and MORET 6, with different cross section libraries, for configurations 1 through 6. MORET 6
 was used with the JEFF3.3 and ENDF/B-VIII.0 libraries, while MCNP 6.3.0 was used with the
 ENDF/B-VIII.0 library. The results of these comparisons are presented in Table 1.2 below.

 Keff
 Configuration MORET 6/JEFF3.3 MORET 6/ENDF8.0 MCNP6.3.0/ENDF8.0
              1            1.00015(10)               0.99835(10)              0.99781(2)
              2            0.99988(10)               0.99785(10)              0.99744(2)
              3            0.99800(10)               0.99620(10)              0.99578(2)
              4            0.99507(10)               0.99347(10)              0.99285(2)
              5            0.98914(10)               0.98750(10)              0.98684(2)
              6            0.97722(10)               0.97552(10)              0.97495(2)

 Table 2: Comparison of kef f results for MORET 6 using JEFF 3.3 and ENDF 8.0 libraries, and
 MCNP 6.3.0 with the ENDF 8.0 library.

 The keff calculated using MORET 6 with JEFF 3.3 is significantly larger than that obtained with
 the ENDF 8.0 library, with an average difference of more than 150 pcm, and at more than 15 sigmas
 apart. In particular, configuration 1, the most reactive configuration, is found to be super-critical
 when using the JEFF 3.3 library.

 Fixed Source Analog Approach

 Analog calculations were performed using MORET 6 coupled with the same two libraries: ENDF
 8.0 and JEFF 3.3. Note that JEFF 3.3 cannot be used for case 1, as this configuration is supercritical
 according to the library. In addition, MORET 6 also uses the LLNL Fission Library [3] to estimate
 the multiplicity and energy of neutrons in both spontaneous and induced fissions. ENDF7.1 library
 is used to calculate the emission time of delayed neutrons.

 Neutrons are tracked on a history-by-history basis rather than chronologically. Neutrons gener-
 ated by histories that start before the set end time of 3600 s (same as the measurement time) are not
 truncated. Neutron events occurring beyond 3600 s are shifted to the beginning of the simulation
 to account for the build-up time of the neutron population (no neutrons emitted at t<0 s). This is
 achieved by applying a modulo operator. This method may also allow for the generation of data sets
 with varying intensities of α-n reactions and spontaneous fissions, as well as different simulated times.

 Only neutrons that interact in the active area of the four 3He detectors surrounding the core
 are taken into account. The intensities of the intrinsic neutron source (spontaneous fissions and (α,
 n) reactions) are taken from Section 3.4. The benchmark quantities, αp and Ysat are determined
 following the same approach used to analyze the experimental data.

 Figure 1 presents a comparison of experimental and simulated Y(τ ) curves using MORET 6 with
 the ENDF 8.0 library. A relatively good agreement is observed for the lower multiplicity configura-
 tions. However, for the highest multiplication configurations, 2 and 1 respectively, a divergence is
 apparent for large time width gates, where the contribution of delayed neutrons becomes significant.
